# Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon Agentic Environments with Dense Progress Rewards

Wenbo Hu

Affiliation: Adobe Research

Affiliation: University of California, Los Angeles

 [https://tev-agent.github.io](https://tev-agent.github.io/)    Shraman
Pramanick Affiliation: Adobe Research    Krishna Kumar Singh
Affiliation: Adobe Research   

Nanyun Peng  Kai-Wei Chang  Yong Jae Lee

Affiliation: Adobe Research

Affiliation: University of California, Los Angeles

 [https://tev-agent.github.io](https://tev-agent.github.io/)

###### Abstract

Long-horizon agents have drawn growing attention for their ability to
work autonomously—from hours to days—on economically valuable tasks, a
capability essential to automated discovery and recursive
self-improvement (RSI). Yet open-source models still struggle on
long-horizon tasks, largely for lack of both training data and a working
recipe, and scaling both is hard: long, complex tasks are difficult to
curate and verify, and their rewards are sparse. We address both. First,
we introduce Tev-Gym, the first long-horizon agent training dataset with
verifiable, dense, progressive rewards—$`1{,}170`$ verified task
environments across $`7`$ domains. It is built on a _latent-world-first_
approach that first constructs a _latent world_: a complete and correct
hidden specification of the task domain, workflow, and expected state
after each step. Each task is then progressively rendered from this
specification into an agent-facing environment, enabling controllable
complexity and a natural progress metric. Second, we turn this metric
into a training recipe: via progress-potential reward shaping (PPRS), we
convert progress into a dense per-step reward that improves training
efficiency and makes otherwise-intractable tasks trainable. Training
with this recipe yields Tev-27B, which achieves substantial gains on the
Agents’ Last Exam computing and math sciences domains (ALE-CM), matching
GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5 at Pass@5. Despite training only on
ALE-related data, it generalizes to the Terminal-Bench (TB) 3.0 and 4.0
subsets, surpassing GLM 5.2 and Gemini 3.7 Flash, and reaches Claude
Opus 4.8-level performance on middle-horizon tasks such as TB 2.1.

| --- |

![Refer to caption](2610.18868v1/teaser_ours_v12.png)

Figure 1: We demonstrate Tev’s results on ALE-CM, and generalization to
Terminal-Bench (TB) 3.0-CM, 4.0-CM, and 2.1 on the left and a summary of
our latent-world-first data synthesis approach on the right. Tev
showcases significant performance gains across two model scales and
Tev-27B matches GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5 at Pass@5 on ALE-CM.

## 1 Introduction

Recent progress in large language models (LLMs) has shifted attention
toward long-horizon agents ([OpenAI, 2026](#bib.bib80); [Anthropic,
2026](#bib.bib81); [Moonshot AI, 2026](#bib.bib82)), which can work
autonomously for hours to days on complex, economically valuable
tasks ([Sun et al., 2026](#bib.bib65); [Styles et al.,
2024](#bib.bib83); [Zhu et al., 2026a](#bib.bib85)). Long-horizon tasks,
such as solving open problems in mathematics and science, conducting
automated machine learning research, and advancing coding and security
systems, are fundamental to automated discovery and recursive
self-improvement (RSI).

Figure 2: All models on the Terminal-Bench 4.0 “CM” subset (TB 4.0-CM),
ranked by Pass@1. Tev-320B and Tev-27B are trained with our recipe from
GLM-5.3-Flash and Qwen3.8-27B, respectively; each arrow gives the
relative gain over its base.

Despite these promising breakthroughs, coding-agent progress in the
open-source community has focused on shorter- and mid-horizon tasks,
scaled via large agentic training corpora such as SWE-Smith ([Yang et
al., 2025a](#bib.bib31)), TMAX ([Ivison et al., 2026](#bib.bib29)),
Endless Terminals ([Gandhi et al., 2026](#bib.bib40)), CLI-Gym ([Lin et
al., 2026](#bib.bib41)), SETA ([Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)), and
Terminal-Universe ([Wu et al., 2026](#bib.bib30)). These datasets
contain thousands to tens of thousands of environments, but each
involves only a pull-request-sized fix or a short terminal session, and
their unit-test-based evaluation provides only a terminal pass/fail
signal or the fraction of tests passed. By contrast, genuinely
long-horizon tasks are available only in human-authored
benchmarks ([Merrill et al., 2026](#bib.bib39); [Li et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib27)). These benchmarks contain only tens to a few hundred
instances, each requiring weeks of expert effort. They provide partial
credit only through hand-written checkpoints and exist only as
evaluation sets. Thus, no training corpus of long-horizon tasks exists:
agentic training corpora are large but short-horizon, while long-horizon
tasks exist only as small, human-authored evaluation sets.

Curating long-horizon tasks requires balancing two competing goals:
tasks must be sufficiently long and structurally complex to provide
meaningful training while remaining exactly verifiable ([Zeng et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib66)). Scaling long-horizon environments for training is
even more difficult because rewards are sparse and available only upon
task completion, depriving long-tail tasks of useful training signals.
To address both limitations, we introduce a _latent-world-first_
synthesis approach that begins by constructing a _latent world_, a
complete and correct hidden specification of the task domain. We first
represent the underlying workflow as a directed acyclic graph (DAG)
within a latent world and then progressively render the input files from
that world. This procedure allows us to control task complexity while
retaining every correct intermediate state, which provides a natural
metric for measuring progress and therefore a verifiable dense reward
for our training. Unlike pipelines that ask a language model to generate
the task, inputs, and checker without a true answer, our latent world
serves as an exact answer key for reliable verification.

Long-horizon tasks come with sparse rewards, arriving only at task
completion and submission, which notoriously starves training on
long-tail samples ([Yu et al., 2025](#bib.bib3); [Shen et al.,
2026](#bib.bib23); [Zeng et al., 2026a](#bib.bib66)). Group-relative
policy optimization ([Shao et al., 2024](#bib.bib2), GRPO;) normalizes
rewards within a group of $`G`$ rollouts of the same task, so under
terminal-only reward, a group in which all $`G`$ rollouts fail has zero
advantage everywhere and contributes nothing to the gradient.
Mid-horizon pipelines can afford to discard such groups ([Ivison et al.,
2026](#bib.bib29); [Yu et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)) because they are rare
there; on long-horizon tasks they are most common at the start of
training, when the policy is weakest and signal is needed most. We
therefore introduce progress-potential reward shaping (PPRS) into
reinforcement learning (RL), which makes training efficient and makes
otherwise-intractable tasks trainable. PPRS scores the agent’s workspace
after every turn $`t`$ against the known-correct intermediate states
from our latent world, giving a progress potential
$`\Phi(s_{t})\in[0,1]`$ that rises as the workflow is completed and
falls on regressions. Reward shaped by $`\Phi`$ gives within-group
variance from the first turn, even when no rollout finishes.

Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
summarizes our approach and Tev’s results, and
Figure [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
ranks all models on the Terminal-Bench (TB) 4.0 “CM” subset by Pass@1.
Using our latent-world-first synthesis approach, we introduce Tev-Gym,
composed of 1,170 verified long-horizon task environments across 7
domains (software engineering, data engineering, AI/CS research,
mathematics and operations research, security and forensics,
infrastructure administration, and quantum computing). As illustrated in
Table [1](#S1.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
Tev-Gym’s tasks take a frontier agent a median of 12.4 minutes per
rollout, about 6 times longer than the most time-intensive prior
corpus ([Yang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib31)), and have the lowest pass
rate, demonstrating our training data’s complexity and quality.
Moreover, Tev-Gym can be scaled beyond a fixed collection by coding
agents writing generators that produce new verified environments across
seeds and scale tiers without further model calls.

We introduce Tev-9B and Tev-27B, trained with PPRS on the unit tier and
the full corpus of Tev-Gym, respectively. On ALE-CM, the
computing-and-math split of Agents’ Last Exam ([Sun et al.,
2026](#bib.bib65)), Tev-9B improves Pass@5 by $`83\%`$ over Qwen3.5-9B,
while Tev-27B improves it by $`65\%`$ over Qwen3.8-27B to $`0.28`$,
matching GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5. Despite training only on
ALE-related data, Tev-27B generalizes to the TB 3.0 and 4.0
subsets ([Merrill et al., 2026](#bib.bib39); [Marten et al.,
2026](#bib.bib37); [Marten, 2026](#bib.bib38)), improving Pass@1 by
$`56\%`$ and $`150\%`$, respectively, and outperforming GLM 5.2 and
Gemini 3.7 Flash. On the mid-horizon TB 2.1, Tev-9B improves zero-shot
Pass@1 by $`28\%`$, while Tev-27B reaches $`0.794`$ Pass@1, matching
Claude Opus 4.8-level performance. The recipe also transfers to a larger
base: applying it to GLM-5.3-Flash yields Tev-320B, which raises Pass@1
on the TB 3.0 and 4.0 subsets by $`35\%`$ and $`28\%`$ and, on TB 4.0,
trails only Claude Opus 5, Claude Fable 5, and GPT-5.6 (sol).

Our main contributions are summarized as follows:

- •
  We propose latent-world-first synthesis, a pipeline that constructs
  long-horizon agentic tasks backward from a known-correct world, giving
  exact verification, a controllable difficulty ladder, and a
  progressive reward in the middle.
- •
  We introduce Tev-Gym, the first long-horizon agent training dataset
  with progressive rewards, composed of 1,170 verified long-horizon task
  environments across 7 domains.
- •
  We present Tev-27B, trained on Tev-Gym using PPRS, and demonstrate its
  superior performance on ALE-CM, the TB 3.0 and TB 4.0 subsets, and
  TB 2.1; the same recipe applied to GLM-5.3-Flash gives Tev-320B, which
  improves further.

## 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis

Figure [3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
details the mechanisms of long-horizon environment reconstruction. This
section defines how an environment is synthesized backward from a latent
world
(§[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
and how coverage, difficulty, and correctness are enforced in Tev-Gym
(§[2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Coverage, Calibration, and Qualification ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).

![Refer to caption](2610.18868v1/pip_fig_final_v3.png)

Figure 3: Latent-world-first synthesis, on one example Tev-Gym
environment. This environment’s task is “Retail Warehouse ETL Recovery.
Goal: build a clean star-schema SQLite warehouse from six months of
messy retail data and produce two truthful JSON sidecars.”

### 2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis

Stage 0: task sources. Our training data targets ALE-CM, whose coverage
we group into seven major domains. Within each domain, we randomly
sample specifications and author our own generator around each one. For
details, please refer to
Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1 "A.1 Task Sources and Domain Taxonomy ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

Stage 1: world construction. Each generator is a seeded program. Given a
seed and a scale tier, it procedurally generates the latent world $`W`$,
the complete, correct state of the domain (e.g., a retail warehouse’s
customers, products, and transactions, or a distributed checkpoint’s
tensor shards and topology), and we validate $`W`$ against the
environment’s invariants (unique identities, valid joins, finite
measures). The same step draws a seed-derived _defect plan_: exactly
which records, shards, or fields of $`W`$ will later be corrupted, and
by which declared operator.

Stage 2: workflow graph. We compile the environment’s workflow into a
directed acyclic graph (DAG) of stages with explicit dependencies,
permitted parallelism, and per-stage semantic-work estimates (e.g.,
profile-sources $`\rightarrow`$ reconcile-facts / normalize-dimensions
$`\rightarrow`$ load-facts $`\rightarrow`$ aggregate-validate
$`\rightarrow`$ package-sidecars). The DAG is built by code from the
scale of $`W`$, with no model in the loop. Its critical path, the
longest chain of dependent stages, defines the task’s horizon, and its
stages form the skeleton of the progress potential in
§[3](#S3 "3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

Stage 3: artifact rendering. We _project_ $`W`$ onto the environment’s
declared public interface, materializing the clean input files in their
target wire formats: CSV and TSV partitions, JSON records, SQLite
databases, safetensors shards, stripped binaries, or infrastructure
manifests. Rendering is a pure function of $`W`$, and before proceeding
we assert that it round-trips, i.e., that $`W`$ is recovered exactly
from the rendered files. Together with the invertible corruption of
Stage 4, this makes the agent’s workspace $`s_{0}`$ fully determine
$`W`$, so the correct answer is computable from what the agent actually
sees, and the ground truth is correct by construction.

Stage 4: controlled corruption. We apply the Stage-1 defect plan to the
rendered files, injecting schema drift, duplicate and missing records,
categorical drift, scrambled shard indices, stale caches, and the like
(Figure [3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
shows the three operators of one environment). The corrupted tree
$`s_{0}`$ _is_ the task the agent receives, and the untouched world
$`W`$ _is_ the ground truth. Because rendering round-trips and
corruption is declared and invertible, $`s_{0}`$ fully determines $`W`$:
the correct solution is computable from what the agent sees and is known
exactly without running a solver. More generally, every component of an
environment,

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
E\;=\;\bigl(x,\;s_{0},\;\operatorname{ver}_{W},\;\Phi_{W}\bigr),
``` |  | (1) |

is a function of the same $`W`$: the task prompt $`x`$, the input tree
$`s_{0}`$, the strict pass/fail verifier $`\operatorname{ver}_{W}`$,
which checks the agent’s output files against $`W`$, and the progress
potential $`\Phi_{W}`$ of
§[3](#S3 "3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").
This single anchor prevents *artifact drift* ([Ivanov and Rana,
2026](#bib.bib72)), where an environment, task, and verifier generated
separately each look valid on their own yet describe different problems.

Stages 5 and 6: public specification and hidden suite. We emit the
public contract, the task prompt $`x`$ (input roles, required output
artifacts and their schemas, ordered requirements, authority-precedence
rules, and the permitted toolchain), which is the only description the
agent sees. Outside the agent’s view, we seal a hidden suite used at
verification time: metamorphic transforms, which are
semantics-preserving rewrites that must not change the grade, and
stale-output counterfactuals, which are plausible-but-wrong states that
must fail.

Generalization of latent-world-first synthesis. We focus on the ALE-CM
domains due to resource constraints, but the approach applies to other
ALE domains.
Appendix [A.10](#A1.SS10 "A.10 Three Worked Training Environments ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
walks through three environments built by the pipeline: a multimodal AWS
cost-optimization task with its billing-dashboard screenshot rendered
from our latent world (cloud operations), closed-loop building control
of an EnergyPlus model (engineering), and financial-statement
reconstruction from SEC-style filings (finance).

### 2.2 Coverage, Calibration, and Qualification

Diversity. Every environment is labeled on 19 coverage axes
(Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3 "A.3 Coverage Axes and Covering Array ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
including subdomain, scale, the four horizon axes, workflow stage, tool
capability, artifact role, relation type, and corruption class. The axes
that matter for training are behavioral: changing their value changes
what the agent must repair (corruption class), how (workflow stage, tool
capability), and through which files (artifact role, relation type). For
more details, please see our
Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3 "A.3 Coverage Axes and Covering Array ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

Controllable difficulty. Two controls change a task’s difficulty. The
scale tier (by default *unit*, *small*, or *full*) grows $`W`$
(Appendix [A.4](#A1.SS4 "A.4 Scale Ladder ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))
and lengthens the operational horizon: how much must be read, joined,
and written. The workflow DAG of Stage 2, together with the number of
corruption classes in the defect plan, fixes the dependency depth: how
many stages must be completed, and in which order, before any output can
be right.
Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Measuring Horizon and Difficulty ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
states how we measure horizon and difficulty and reports base-model and
frontier solve rates.

Qualification gates and cleanliness. A synthesized environment is
admitted only if it passes all eight benchmark-independent gates,
checked automatically at generation time and each matched to a named
failure class
(Table [7](#A1.T7 "Table 7 ‣ A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
Three of them back the progress potential of
§[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 A Progress Potential from the Latent World ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"):
all 14 gaming probes must score $`0`$ (*exploit resistance*), the
potential must lie strictly between $`0`$ and $`1`$ on partially correct
states and increase monotonically to $`1`$ along the reference
trajectory (*progress validity*), and the progress scorer, strict
verifier, and reference solvers must have disjoint code closures (*role
independence*).
Appendix [A.6](#A1.SS6 "A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
states every gate formally, together with the verifier-independent
solver audit, and
Appendix [A.9](#A1.SS9 "A.9 Decontamination ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
describes the structural decontamination check.

Figure 4: The progress potential along one group ($`G{=}8`$) of

environment, graded live after every turn. Under terminal-only reward

65k-token context (crosses), and one builds a complete warehouse, so the
rewards vary within the group.

## 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL

The latent world that grades task completion also grades its middle.
This section turns it into a training signal: we quantify why
terminal-only reward starves group-relative RL
(§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Terminal Reward Starves Group-Relative RL ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
define the progress potential every Tev-Gym environment provides
(§[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 A Progress Potential from the Latent World ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
and detail the shaped reward
(§[3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).

### 3.1 Terminal Reward Starves Group-Relative RL

Consider GRPO ([Shao et al., 2024](#bib.bib2)), the estimator we and
most recent agentic RL pipelines use. For a task $`x`$ the policy
samples $`G`$ rollouts $`\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{G}`$ with rewards
$`R_{1},\dots,R_{G}`$, and each rollout is weighted by its
group-normalized advantage

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{A}_{i}\;=\;\frac{R_{i}-\operatorname{mean}(R_{1:G})}{\operatorname{std}(R_{1:G})+\epsilon}.
``` |  | (2) |

With terminal reward $`R_{i}=\mathds{1}[\tau_{i}\ \text{passes}]`$,
where a rollout *passes* when the policy issues the submit action and
the strict verifier $`\operatorname{ver}_{W}`$ accepts the submitted
workspace, a group in which every rollout fails has $`\hat{A}_{i}=0`$
for all $`i`$: the task contributes nothing to the gradient and its
$`G`$ rollouts are wasted. If the policy solves the task with
probability $`p`$, the fraction of wasted groups is $`(1-p)^{G}`$:
$`66\%`$ at $`p{=}0.05`$ and $`G{=}8`$, and $`92\%`$ at $`p{=}0.01`$
(Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Dense Reward Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
On long-horizon tasks, where $`p`$ for an untrained open model is
roughly one in ten or lower
(§[4](#S4 "4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
dropping or resampling such groups ([Ivison et al., 2026](#bib.bib29);
[Yu et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)) discards most of the batch. A group with a
single success fares only slightly better. Its one positive rollout is
contrasted with $`G-1`$ failures that all receive the same negative
advantage, so the gradient cannot tell a near-miss from a rollout that
made no progress.

### 3.2 A Progress Potential from the Latent World

Every Tev-Gym environment provides a *progress contract*: a set of nodes
$`V`$, each a checkable state of the output workspace derived from the
workflow DAG of
§[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
with weights $`w_{v}>0`$, $`\sum_{v}w_{v}=1`$, and prerequisite sets
$`\mathrm{pre}(v)`$ (example in
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Dense Reward Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
A *partial scorer* $`c_{v}(s)\in[0,1]`$ grades the agent’s current
workspace $`s`$ on node $`v`$ directly against the latent world. Credit
is prerequisite-gated: $`\tilde{c}_{v}(s)`$ is the lowest partial score
among $`v`$ and all its direct and indirect prerequisites. The
potential, written $`\Phi`$ for $`\Phi_{W}`$ of
Eq. ([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
is the weighted sum:

|     |                                                               |     |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Phi(s)\;=\;\sum_{v\in V}w_{v}\,\tilde{c}_{v}(s)\;\in\;[0,1].
       ```                                                            |     | (3) |

Preventing reward hacking. Three properties make $`\Phi`$ a usable
training signal, each enforced by a qualification gate
(§[2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Coverage, Calibration, and Qualification ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")):
it is *anchored* (exploit resistance), with the untouched public tree
and every gaming probe scoring $`\Phi=0`$ and $`\Phi=1`$ exactly when
the strict verifier accepts the workspace; *monotone* (progress
validity), rising as nodes are completed and falling when completed work
is destroyed; and *independent* (role independence), computed by a
scorer whose code closure is disjoint from those of the verifier and the
reference solvers, so a defect in one grader is not inherited by the
other; none of this grading code is shipped in the public tree.

Scalable reward design. Because the latent world is defined before task
generation, every correct intermediate state can be rendered directly
and evaluated by $`c_{v}`$. This provides exact and deterministic
progress feedback for every generated seed and scale without the human
step labels used by process reward models ([Lightman et al.,
2024](#bib.bib6)), a trained reward model, or a language-model judge.

### 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS)

Let $`s_{0},\dots,s_{T}`$ be the workspace states along a rollout
$`\tau`$, with $`s_{0}`$ the untouched public tree, so
$`\Phi(s_{0})=0`$. As in potential-based reward shaping ([Ng et al.,
1999](#bib.bib5), PBRS;), the per-step shaping reward is the change in
potential, $`r_{t}=\Phi(s_{t})-\Phi(s_{t-1})`$, and the rollout reward
is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
R(\tau)\;=\;\mathds{1}[\tau\ \text{passes}]\;+\;\lambda\sum_{t=1}^{T}r_{t}\;=\;\mathds{1}[\tau\ \text{passes}]\;+\;\lambda\,\Phi(s_{T}),
``` |  | (4) |

with $`\lambda=0.25`$ in every run
(Appendix [C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Training Recipe and Hyperparameters ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
Unlike PBRS, which zeroes the terminal potential so that optimal
policies are unchanged, PPRS keeps $`\lambda\Phi(s_{T})`$ and
deliberately changes the objective
(Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Dense Reward Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
Submission is an action, not a state: $`\Phi`$ grades workspaces, and
the terminal term grades the workspace the policy submits. A rollout
that exhausts its turn or token budget without submitting is not graded
terminally and earns $`\lambda\Phi(s_{T})`$ alone; this is how the
complete but unsubmitted rollout of
Figure [4](#S2.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 2.2 Coverage, Calibration, and Qualification ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
reaches $`\Phi=1`$ with terminal reward $`0`$. Because $`\Phi`$ is a
function of the workspace alone, $`R(\tau)`$ depends on the rollout only
through its final state $`s_{T}`$: two rollouts that reach the same
workspace by different routes, in a different order of steps or with a
different implementation, receive the same reward. The telescoping form
also keeps the terminal reward’s ranking at the top: since
$`\Phi\leq 1`$, any passing rollout earns $`1+\lambda`$, strictly more
than any failing one, so no single rollout is rewarded for trading
completion against partial progress; for stochastic policies,
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Dense Reward Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
bounds when the preference can reverse.
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Dense Reward Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
states what else is and is not preserved.

Substituting
Eq. ([4](#S3.E4 "Equation 4 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))
into
Eq. ([2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 3.1 Terminal Reward Starves Group-Relative RL ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))
shows why this repairs the starvation of GRPO. A group in which no
rollout passes now has rewards $`R_{i}=\lambda\Phi(s_{T}^{(i)})`$, where
$`s_{T}^{(i)}`$ is the final workspace of $`\tau_{i}`$. These differ
whenever the rollouts progressed to different depths, so the group’s
advantages are non-zero and rank the rollouts by progress;
Figure [4](#S2.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 2.2 Coverage, Calibration, and Qualification ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
shows one such group, and
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Dense Reward Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
the 24 groups we probed.

Table 2: Main results on ALE-CM, computing and math science domains of
Agents’ Last Exam (ALE). Each cell reports the mean graded score (S@1)
and the fraction of tasks fully solved (P@1), averaged over 5 rollouts
per task, together with their best-of-5 counterparts (S@5, P@5).

## 4 Experiments

Our main experiments ask three questions. Does RL on Tev-Gym improve
open models on the long-horizon tasks it was built for, do the gains
generalize to tasks of even longer horizon, and does the model keep its
general coding competence on a standard mid-horizon benchmark
(§[4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))?
Finally,
§[4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Ablation Study ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
ablates our latent-world-first data and the dense reward approaches,
separately.

### 4.1 Setup

Training. We train Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.8-27B using DPPO ([Qi et al.,
2026](#bib.bib84)) with the reward in our
Eq. ([4](#S3.E4 "Equation 4 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
scaling the training data together with the model: the 9B model trains
on a 264-task subset of Tev-Gym from the *unit* difficulty tier, which
is better suited for learning at this model scale, while the 27B model
trains on the full corpus. We choose $`G{=}24`$ rollouts for each of 8
tasks per step, a response budget of 65,536 tokens per rollout (16,384
per turn), and at most 64 tool calls, in a sandbox that exposes a single
bash tool. Our 27B model is trained on 4 nodes of B200 GPUs or 8 nodes
of A100 GPUs for about 4 days. For more details, refer to
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").
To test whether the recipe transfers to a larger base model, we also
train GLM-5.3-Flash with the same recipe, obtaining Tev-320B, which we
evaluate on the TB 3.0 and 4.0 subsets.

Evaluation. Long-horizon agent evaluation tasks are scarce and costly,
with each requiring up to eight hours to evaluate. Our in-domain suite,
ALE-CM, is the computing-and-math split of Agents’ Last Exam ([Sun et
al., 2026](#bib.bib65)), listed in
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
(Table [12](#A3.T12 "Table 12 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
On TB 3.0 and 4.0 ([Merrill et al., 2026](#bib.bib39); [Marten et al.,
2026](#bib.bib37); [Marten, 2026](#bib.bib38)) we evaluate the “CM”
subsets (22 and 18 tasks). To form them we asked Claude to judge, for
every TB 3.0 task, whether it belongs to the computing-and-math domain,
even if only slightly; the selection and the full task lists are given
in
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
(Table [13](#A3.T13 "Table 13 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
We compare leaderboard model performance on the full ALE, TB 3.0, and
TB 4.0 benchmarks with performance on our corresponding subsets. The
results show that our splits are representative of full-set performance,
with Pearson correlations of $`r=0.96`$ for TB 3.0 P@1 and $`r=0.97`$
for TB 4.0 P@1
(Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards");
Tables [14](#A3.T14 "Table 14 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
[15](#A3.T15 "Table 15 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
and [16](#A3.T16 "Table 16 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).

Train/eval decontamination. We measure lexical leakage per evaluation
set: no environment of Tev-Gym shares a 13-gram or an 8-gram with any
task of ALE-CM, TB 3.0, TB 4.0 or TB 2.1, and the median
nearest-neighbor prompt Jaccard of the evaluation tasks to the dataset
is $`0.13`$ on TB 3.0/4.0 and $`0.22`$ on ALE-CM, against
$`0.24`$–$`0.25`$ and $`0.28`$ for tasks of the same benchmark compared
with one another. For more details, refer to
Appendix [A.9](#A1.SS9 "A.9 Decontamination ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
Table [10](#A1.T10 "Table 10 ‣ A.9 Decontamination ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

### 4.2 Main Results

Results on long-horizon tasks. As shown in
Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
Tev-9B improves mean S@1 by 16% and nearly doubles its best-of-5 solve
rate over Qwen3.5-9B, while Tev-27B produces $`33\%`$ more full
solutions than Qwen3.8-27B. Trained on the full corpus rather than the
unit tier, Tev-27B shows larger absolute gains than Tev-9B on every
metric and surpasses Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B under the same harness on P@1.
Although Claude Fable 5 reaches only $`0.33`$ P@5, training raises the
27B model from $`0.17`$ to $`0.28`$, reaching $`85\%`$ of the frontier
score and closing more than two-thirds of the original gap.

Table 4: Terminal-Bench (TB) 2.1 results. Frontier-reference rows are
taken from the official TB 2.1 leaderboard (5 trials, 89 tasks).

Results on longer-horizon tasks. The TB 3.0 and 4.0 “CM” subsets are
even more challenging, with each task requiring eight hours of expert
work. Our 27B base model fully solves only $`9\%`$ and $`4\%`$ of
attempted tasks, respectively. As shown in
Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
Tev-27B raises P@1 from $`0.04`$ to $`0.10`$ ($`2.5\times`$) on TB 4.0
and from $`0.09`$ to $`0.14`$ ($`+56\%`$) on TB 3.0. Notably, Tev-27B
surpasses GLM 5.2, the strongest open-source model on the leaderboard.
Compared with frontier models, however, a substantial gap remains.
Claude Opus 5, the strongest agent on both suites, achieves P@1 scores
of $`0.53`$ on TB 3.0 and $`0.52`$ on TB 4.0, suggesting that stronger
open-source base models are needed to fully benefit from our
high-quality but challenging Tev-Gym.

Scaling the base model. Consistent with this, the recipe carries over to
a stronger base. Applying it to GLM-5.3-Flash yields Tev-320B, whose P@1
rises from $`0.20`$ to $`0.27`$ on TB 3.0 and from $`0.29`$ to $`0.37`$
on TB 4.0, with gains in every category on both suites and the largest
on TB 3.0 security ($`0.22`$ to $`0.45`$). The improvement is
concentrated in P@1: P@5 gains $`0.05`$ on TB 3.0 and is unchanged on
TB 4.0, so training mainly makes the model solve the tasks it can
already reach more reliably rather than unlocking new ones. Tev-320B is
the strongest open-source model on both subsets; on TB 3.0 it edges past
Grok 4.5 and Claude Opus 4.8, and on TB 4.0 it trails only Claude
Opus 5, Claude Fable 5, and GPT-5.6 (sol) in P@1
(Figure [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).

Results on middle-horizon tasks. We next evaluate our model on TB 2.1
(Table [4](#S4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
a standard mid-horizon terminal benchmark. Despite zero data targeting
TB 2.1, our model shows strong zero-shot performance. Tev-9B
significantly outperforms its base after training, raising P@1 by
$`28\%`$ and P@5 by $`19\%`$, showing that training on long-horizon
tasks also improves performance on standard mid-horizon work. Our 27B
model achieves P@1 $`0.794`$ and P@5 $`0.884`$, a smaller but consistent
gain over its base and on par with Claude Opus 4.8, though still behind
Claude Fable 5 and GPT-6 Astra.

Qualitative results.
Figure [5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.3 Ablation Study ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
traces the key decision points of four rollouts on the
k8s_payment_api_root_cause_analysis task: every agent finds the same
root causes, but Tev-27B and Claude Fable 5 ground the liveness-probe
cause in the measurement that explains it and judge rollback safe,
whereas the base model and GPT-6 Astra cite only the symptoms and
decline rollback, forfeiting the decisive grader component. For more
qualitative examples, see
Appendix [C.3](#A3.SS3 "C.3 Qualitative Examples on ALE-CM ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

### 4.3 Ablation Study

Due to resource constraints, we conduct the ablation study in the 9B
setting of
§[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

Ablation on data curation methods. Our pipeline differs from standard
LLM-synthesized corpora in two key ways: tasks are derived from a
preexisting latent world and its code-compiled workflow DAG, and
verifiers evaluate against this hidden ground truth rather than only
agent-visible files.
Table [5](#S4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ 4.3 Ablation Study ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
shows which components each ablation retains, with GPT-5.6 (sol, xhigh)
replacing the others. *Public-view verifier* ablates only access to
hidden ground truth by asking the model to write a verifier from the
agent’s view. *Prompt-first*, a standard self-instruct baseline, instead
replaces the entire construction pipeline by generating each prompt
before its inputs, verifier, and solution, leaving no latent world or
DAG. The public-view variant causes 88% of sampled groups to be
discarded during training. Because both variants use terminal rewards,
we compare them with the *Sparse* row: neither improves over the base
model in S@1 ($`0.24`$ and $`0.23`$ vs. $`0.25`$), whereas our full
method leads them by $`0.05`$–$`0.06`$ in S@1 and $`0.05`$ in P@5.

Ablation on dense reward. Holding Tev-Gym fixed, we compare our PPRS,
which uses return-to-go $`G_{t}`$ with $`\gamma=1`$ and normalizes
advantages by turn index, against terminal-only sparse reward, PPRS with
per-rollout normalization (*pooled norm.*), and direct additive reward
using $`\Phi(s_{t})`$ with $`\gamma=0`$ ([Yuan et al.,
2026](#bib.bib19)). Our method provides the best balance of performance
and training efficiency, improving S@1 by $`0.04`$ over sparse reward.
Turn-index normalization preserves credit for later turns by avoiding
comparison of their smaller returns against the whole-rollout mean,
while $`\gamma=1`$ propagates progress back to earlier actions. In
contrast, direct additive reward repeatedly rewards high-potential
states without propagating credit, leading to more rollouts exhausting
the context window before submission and producing longer trajectories.
The advantage over sparse reward widens further at 27B, where shaped
reward trains to completion but terminal-only reward produces almost
entirely zero-advantage groups and fails to complete a single optimizer
step.

## 5 Related Work

Synthetic corpora for coding and terminal agents. Recent corpora
typically mine repositories for issues and tests or use language models
to generate tasks such as bug-injected repositories and reconstructed
terminal sessions ([Jimenez et al., 2024](#bib.bib1); [Yang et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib31); [Ivison et al., 2026](#bib.bib29); [Lin et al.,
2026](#bib.bib41)). Other work scales executable tool-use environments
and their success conditions ([Cai et al., 2025](#bib.bib70); [Fang et
al., 2025](#bib.bib57)), although scaling helps only when difficulty is
controlled ([Zhu et al., 2026b](#bib.bib60)). In contrast, our
environments contain long, multi-stage workflows across mathematics and
science, machine learning, coding, and systems; are instantiated by
seeded generator code rather than directly authored by a language model;
and provide a dense progress signal for every task.

Backward construction. Deriving a task from a known solution is an
established family: denoising, controlled error injection, code
mutation, environment rewinding, instruction back-translation, and
procedural puzzle generation all corrupt or invert a single artifact
whose ground truth is already known ([Lewis et al., 2020](#bib.bib43);
[Arocena et al., 2015](#bib.bib63); [Tran et al., 2013](#bib.bib64);
[Yang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib31); [Hariharan et al., 2025](#bib.bib36);
[Lin et al., 2026](#bib.bib41); [Li et al., 2023](#bib.bib44); [Wu et
al., 2024](#bib.bib45); [Stojanovski et al., 2025](#bib.bib46); [Chen et
al., 2025a](#bib.bib47); [Liu et al., 2025](#bib.bib48); [Li et al.,
2025](#bib.bib49)). We instead corrupt a rendering of an entire latent
world, so the task, verifier, and progress signal are all functions of
that one world rather than separately generated factors that can drift
apart ([Zeng et al., 2026a](#bib.bib66); [Ivanov and Rana,
2026](#bib.bib72)).

Sparse reward and dense signal in agentic RL. Existing methods avoid
uninformative rollout groups by filtering identical rewards, selecting
tasks near the policy’s current ability, or adding expert prefixes ([Yu
et al., 2025](#bib.bib3); [Florensa et al., 2018](#bib.bib76); [Zhang et
al., 2025c](#bib.bib10)). Other approaches provide denser feedback
through learned judges ([Cui et al., 2025](#bib.bib14); [Choudhury,
2025](#bib.bib13)) or proxy signals such as test-pass rates ([Du et al.,
2025](#bib.bib22); [Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib23); [Yang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib24)), but these require additional models or capture only
terminal outcomes. Our potential $`\Phi`$ instead measures exact
progress and regression against the latent world at every workflow
stage, without requiring a critic or language-model judge. For full
related work, please refer to
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Extended Related Work ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

## 6 Conclusion

We introduce a latent-world-first approach for scaling verifiable
long-horizon agent training. Each task is built backward from a
known-correct latent world, which is rendered into interrelated files,
modified through controlled corruptions, and reused for exact
verification and dense progress rewards. We instantiate this approach in
Tev-Gym, a collection of 1,170 verified environments across seven
domains, and show that training improves both 9B and 27B open models on
ALE-CM, with gains transferring to Terminal-Bench tasks of different
horizons and to a 320B base model. One limitation of our current study
is its focus on synthetic terminal environments, and the hardest
benchmarks still show a gap between our models and leading frontier
agents. Supporting new workflows also requires authoring and validating
additional environment components. Future work could simplify this
process, expand domain coverage, and combine the resulting data with
stronger open models, making more professional workflows available for
reproducible RL without human step labels or language-model judges.

### AI use statement

We used generative AI tools for the following tasks that require
disclosure. *Generating synthetic data and implementing methods:* coding
agents helped author the generators of Tev-Gym, that is, the environment
specifications and their reference solvers, strict verifiers, and
progress scorers
(§[2](#S2 "2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards");
Appendix [A.2](#A1.SS2 "A.2 Determinism, Reproducibility, and Generation Cost ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
Once authored, a generator is a deterministic seeded program, so no
language model participates when an environment is generated, verified,
or rewarded; every generator passed the qualification gates of
Appendix [A.6](#A1.SS6 "A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
and the authors reviewed each specification. *Qualitative data
analysis:* Claude helped judge which TB 3.0 and 4.0 tasks relate to the
computing-and-math domain of our training data, which defines our “CM”
evaluation subsets;
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
gives the procedure and the resulting task lists, and
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
checks that the subsets track full-benchmark performance. Coding
assistants also helped write evaluation and analysis scripts.
Separately, language models appear in our experiments as objects of
study rather than as aids to the authors: GPT-5.6 (sol) builds the two
data-ablation baselines
(§[4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Ablation Study ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
Gemini 3.6 Flash is the fixed evaluator of the corpus difficulty
comparison
(Appendix [A.8](#A1.SS8 "A.8 Comparison Protocol ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
and frontier models are evaluated as baselines. No generative model
grades any task or produces any training reward. Among tasks with
recommended disclosure, we used generative AI tools to search and
summarize related literature and to draft and edit parts of the text.
Translation, surveys, interviews, and transcription are not applicable
to this work. We have reviewed all AI-assisted work, and all claims,
numbers, and citations were checked by the authors. We take
responsibility for the final content of this work, including text,
claims, and artifacts produced with the aid of generative AI.

### Ethics statement

Tev-Gym is fully synthetic: every environment is generated procedurally
from a seed, contains no data collected from people, and involves no
human subjects, so the corpus encodes no private records or proprietary
code. Task specifications are drawn from public benchmarks and reference
material
(Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1 "A.1 Task Sources and Domain Taxonomy ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
but every artifact an agent sees is generated by the environment itself,
and third-party benchmarks and corpora used for evaluation and
comparison are used under their published licenses
(Appendix [A.8](#A1.SS8 "A.8 Comparison Protocol ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
The environments simulate professional workflows such as data
reconciliation, checkpoint consolidation, systems administration, and
the analysis of synthetic binaries and packet captures in the style of
capture-the-flag exercises. Each declares its toolchain in its public
contract, the agent’s workspace is seeded only with its public tree, and
no task targets real software, systems, or people. Improving open-weight
agents on long-horizon terminal tasks carries the general dual-use
considerations of more capable coding agents; our security-flavored
tasks are analysis and forensics exercises over synthetic artifacts, the
same class of task already present in public terminal-agent benchmarks.
Because part of the training mix is built against the interfaces of
ALE-CM, we measure train/evaluation overlap for every evaluation set and
report it with its calibration
(§[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards");
Appendix [A.9](#A1.SS9 "A.9 Decontamination ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
Frontier baselines run in their native agent harnesses, the setting most
favorable to them
(Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
and we discuss the limitations of our results in
§[6](#S6 "6 Conclusion ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

### Reproducibility statement

We have written the paper and appendix so that every result can be
reproduced from them. *Data.* Every environment of Tev-Gym is a
deterministic, model-free function of its generator, seed, and scale
tier
(§[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards");
Appendix [A.2](#A1.SS2 "A.2 Determinism, Reproducibility, and Generation Cost ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
so the corpus can be regenerated exactly, at a median of 30 s of CPU
time per environment and with no model calls. The generation stages are
specified in
§[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards");
task sources and the domain taxonomy in
Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1 "A.1 Task Sources and Domain Taxonomy ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
(Table [6](#A1.T6 "Table 6 ‣ A.1 Task Sources and Domain Taxonomy ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"));
the coverage policy in
Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3 "A.3 Coverage Axes and Covering Array ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards");
the scale ladder and the measurement of horizon and difficulty in
Appendices [A.4](#A1.SS4 "A.4 Scale Ladder ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
and [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Measuring Horizon and Difficulty ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards");
the qualification gates in
Appendix [A.6](#A1.SS6 "A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
(Table [7](#A1.T7 "Table 7 ‣ A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"));
and per-domain statistics in
Appendix [A.7](#A1.SS7 "A.7 Dataset Statistics ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
(Table [8](#A1.T8 "Table 8 ‣ A.7 Dataset Statistics ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
Appendix [A.10](#A1.SS10 "A.10 Three Worked Training Environments ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
walks through three complete training environments, each with its public
view and the ground truth sealed behind it, so the construction can be
followed end to end (the listings are excerpts of the package files).
*Reward and training.* The progress potential and the shaped reward are
fully specified by
Eqs. ([3](#S3.E3 "Equation 3 ‣ 3.2 A Progress Potential from the Latent World ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))
and ([4](#S3.E4 "Equation 4 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
All three base models are publicly available and DPPO is a published
objective;
§[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
states the corpus each model is trained on, and
Table [11](#A3.T11 "Table 11 ‣ C.1 Training Recipe and Hyperparameters ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
lists the hyperparameters shared by every run. Each ablation changes a
single component of this recipe. *Evaluation.* All evaluation benchmarks
are public.
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
gives the harnesses, turn and token budgets, number of rollouts, and
metric definitions;
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
lists every task in ALE-CM and in the TB 3.0/4.0 “CM” subsets
(Tables [12](#A3.T12 "Table 12 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
and [13](#A3.T13 "Table 13 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"));
and
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
shows that the subsets track full-benchmark performance. The difficulty
comparison of
Table [1](#S1.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
follows
Appendix [A.8](#A1.SS8 "A.8 Comparison Protocol ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
which fixes the evaluator, harness, sampling budget, and subsample seed,
and train/evaluation overlap is measured with the protocol of
Appendix [A.9](#A1.SS9 "A.9 Decontamination ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").
````
