````math
## Appendix A Data Construction Details

This appendix expands the condensed description in
§[2](#S2 "2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

### A.1 Task Sources and Domain Taxonomy

Tev-Gym’s generators target the seven computing-and-math subdomains of
Agents’ Last Exam ([Sun et al., 2026](#bib.bib65)), which constitute
ALE-CM and which we refer to throughout as Tev-Gym’s seven domains:
software engineering (9.1), data and analytics engineering (9.2), AI
engineering, safety and CS research (9.3), mathematical and operations
research (9.4), cybersecurity and digital forensics (9.5),
infrastructure engineering and cloud operations (9.6), and quantum
computing (9.7).
Table [6](#A1.T6 "Table 6 ‣ A.1 Task Sources and Domain Taxonomy ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
gives the scope of each and the deterministic signals it is verified by;
subdomain ids and names are taken from the ALE taxonomy and the scope
summaries follow it, reconstructed from O^(\*)NET/SOC 2018 and the
released ALE task specifications. The coverage policy of
Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3 "A.3 Coverage Axes and Covering Array ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
requires every domain to be populated by at least two generators.

Sourcing. For every domain we assemble a pool of real task
specifications: specifications mined from the internet, MLE-bench (82
Kaggle competitions), or, for domain 9.7 (quantum computing), which no
benchmark suite covers, public reference material (MQT Bench’s circuit
suite, Qiskit Experiments manuals, TeNPy’s DMRG examples). From this
pool we randomly sample specifications and author our own generator
around each selection: every generator is a fresh procedural design of
our own, drawing on the sampled specification for structure and
difficulty only, and never reproducing its prompt, input, answer, or
grader text. A feasibility filter first restricts candidates to
specifications whose ground truth is knowable by construction and
executable batch/CPU-only, excluding live-service, vision/audio,
heavy-toolchain, GPU-kernel, and open-ended “improve this” tasks; a
similarity gate then compares every new generator’s code, specification,
and generated data with all already-registered generators and rejects
near-duplicates; and an adversarial review pass attacks each accepted
generator’s specification for hidden shortcuts, too short a horizon, or
a disguised source before implementation.

|  |  |
|----|----|
|  Subdomain |  Scope |
|  9.1 Software Engineering |  Feature implementation, debugging, refactoring, and test authoring across real codebases, turning issues and specs into working, tested code changes. *Verified by:* test-suite pass (gate then score), behavioral correctness under fixed inputs, and no regression in existing tests. |
|  9.2 Data & Analytics Engineering |  ETL/ELT pipelines, recommender and ranking systems, feature engineering, and analytics workflows, turning raw data into validated datasets, features, and model outputs. *Verified by:* output values exact or within tolerance, schema validity (gate), metric reproduction, and feature-parity against a reference. |
|  9.3 AI Engineering, Safety & CS Research |  Model training and evaluation pipelines, research-paper reproduction, checkpoint and config management, and alignment/safety experiments, turning specs and code into reproduced results. *Verified by:* reproduced metrics within tolerance (FID, accuracy, etc.), config and alignment exactness, artifact validity, and deterministic-under-seed behavior. |
|  9.4 Mathematical & Operations Research |  Combinatorial optimization, game-theoretic equilibrium computation, applied mathematics and number theory, and probabilistic estimation and filtering, implemented from specifications. *Verified by:* objective or solution exact or within tolerance, an optimality/feasibility certificate (gate), and reproducibility under a fixed seed. |
|  9.5 Cybersecurity & Digital Forensics |  Reverse engineering and malware-config extraction, network and packet triage, crackme and key-generation analysis, and incident forensics, turning binaries and captures into extracted artifacts and findings. *Verified by:* extracted-value exactness against a reference (hash/string), finding-set precision and recall, and decoded-artifact correctness (gate). |
|  9.6 Infrastructure Engineering & Cloud Operations |  Kubernetes migration and configuration, root-cause analysis of production incidents, OS and security hardening, and infrastructure-as-code, turning infrastructure specs and incidents into working, secured systems. *Verified by:* the system’s world-state (resources/health) under fixed inputs, config correctness against a reference, incident-cause exactness, and policy-check pass (gate). |
|  9.7 Quantum Computing |  Quantum-circuit construction and simulation, quantum-error-correction code analysis, and circuit-level noise simulation, turning circuit and code specs into measured outcomes and logical-error estimates. *Verified by:* statistics and error-rates within tolerance against a reference, reproducibility under a fixed seed, and circuit/structure validity. |

Table 6: The seven computing-and-math subdomains of ALE-CM that Tev-Gym
spans. Subdomains (9.1–9.7) of the Computing & Math domain of Agents’
Last Exam ([Sun et al., 2026](#bib.bib65)), which we refer to as
Tev-Gym’s seven domains. Ids and names are taken from the ALE taxonomy;
scope summaries follow it, reconstructed from O^(\*)NET/SOC 2018 and the
released ALE task specifications. “Verified by” names the deterministic
signals we build the strict verifier and progress potential around.

### A.2 Determinism, Reproducibility, and Generation Cost

Every stage of
§[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
is a pure, seeded function, and no language model participates in
generation. Each generator’s code is authored once by coding agents and
reviewed by the authors (see AI use statement); running it is
model-free. An environment is therefore fully reproducible from its
generator, seed, and scale tier, and two seeds of the same generator
yield provably distinct worlds (the six-seed diversity gate of
Appendix [A.6](#A1.SS6 "A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
The cost of an environment therefore splits in two. A one-time authoring
cost per generator is amortized over every seed and scale tier at which
it is instantiated. The marginal cost is seconds of compute: producing
one environment, including running both reference solvers, sealing the
hidden suite, and previewing the progress potential $`\Phi_{W}`$, takes
a median of 30 s of CPU time (90th percentile 82 s) and no model calls,
against hours of expert time per hand-authored task. Each environment
declares its toolchain (e.g., python3, sqlite3) in its public contract,
and the agent’s workspace is seeded only with its public tree.

### A.3 Coverage Axes and Covering Array

Every environment is labeled on 19 axes, the required axes of the corpus
coverage policy: curriculum layer, subdomain, generator revision, scale
tier, the four horizon axes (semantic, action, operational,
verification), workflow stage, tool capability, artifact role, relation
type, corruption class, source kind, license class, reward component,
observed reward band, outcome category, and failure category. The corpus
must satisfy a strength-2 covering array over these axes: for any two
axes, every valid pair of their values co-occurs in at least one
admitted environment. Coverage is extended greedily by choosing the
generator, seed, and scale tier that fill the largest number of
currently uncovered pairs, so “diverse” is a checkable property of the
corpus rather than a claim. Pairwise coverage is what separates enforced
from nominal diversity and rules out *template locking* ([Zeng et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib66)): a corpus can be balanced on every axis taken alone
while its axis values only ever appear in a few fixed combinations; a
strength-2 covering array rules this out for every pair of axes.
Coverage is checked on admitted environments only, after the gates of
Appendix [A.6](#A1.SS6 "A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").
Appendix [A.7](#A1.SS7 "A.7 Dataset Statistics ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
reports the resulting balance across domains and capability categories.

Multi-format artifacts. Text-only tasks under-represent real terminal
work, so each environment ships concrete, heterogeneous artifacts
inspected through standard tooling: relational databases, columnar and
delimited text, serialized model shards, compiled binaries, and service
manifests. Some environments are also multimodal: part of the latent
world is rendered as an image, such as the billing-dashboard screenshot
of Example 1
(Appendix [A.10](#A1.SS10 "A.10 Three Worked Training Environments ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).

Per-seed world variation. Re-seeding a generator varies entity
identities, values, layouts, tensor names, and the placement of every
corruption, so no two environments share a public tree
(Appendix [A.6](#A1.SS6 "A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
This is the mechanism behind both scale and decontamination.

### A.4 Scale Ladder

Each generator exposes a scale axis that tunes the size of the world.
For the warehouse environment the tiers run from 30 customers and 48
transactions per month (small) to 480 customers and 960 transactions per
month (large), with proportional growth in duplicates and missing
values; for the checkpoint-consolidation environment the ladder is the
shard count crossed with the parallelism topology (pure tensor
parallelism up to combined tensor-, pipeline-, and expert-parallel
layouts). Because tasks are defined over the workflow DAG, typically
five to six critical stages with cross-stage dependencies, the action,
operational, and verification horizons grow with the tier as
first-class, controllable quantities. We sample across the ladder so
that the corpus is neither uniformly trivial nor uniformly unsolvable.
For the generators instantiated at all three scale tiers, the median
input tree grows from 17 files and 51 KB (unit) to 20 files and 168 KB
(small) and 28 files and 586 KB (full) while the workflow DAG is
unchanged.

### A.5 Measuring Horizon and Difficulty

We quote the critical path of the workflow DAG (Stage 2 of
§[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
not the observed rollout length $`T`$, as the structural measure of
horizon, because it does not inflate with an agent’s detours. The scale
tier and the dependency depth of the DAG are generation controls, not
difficulty scores; difficulty is relative to a learner $`\pi`$, so we
report it as verified success under a declared harness,
$`p_{\pi}(E)=\Pr_{\tau\sim\pi(\cdot\mid E)}\bigl[\operatorname{ver}_{W}(s_{T})=1\bigr]`$.
Appendix [A.7](#A1.SS7 "A.7 Dataset Statistics ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
reports this success rate for an untrained Qwen3.8-27B under the
training harness and for a fixed frontier evaluator
(Table [8](#A1.T8 "Table 8 ‣ A.7 Dataset Statistics ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
Where $`p_{\pi}(E)`$ saturates at zero, the progress potential
$`\Phi_{W}(s_{T})`$ of
§[3](#S3 "3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
still resolves difficulty; this is the regime the dense reward is built
for.
Table [1](#S1.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
positions Tev-Gym against recent agentic corpora.

### A.6 Qualification Gates

A synthesized environment is admitted only if it passes every gate
below. All gates are benchmark-independent; they are enforced while each
generator is authored and checked automatically when each environment is
generated.

- •
  Reward causality. Each reward component $`c`$ must lose value when its
  cause is mutated in the workspace:
  $`c(\mu_{c}(s^{\star}))<c(s^{\star})`$ for a passing workspace
  $`s^{\star}`$ and its cause-mutation $`\mu_{c}`$.
- •
  Exploit resistance. A probe $`s^{(k)}`$ for every one of 14 gaming
  strategies (empty output, minimal schema-valid output, copied input,
  single-seed hard-coding, path traversal, private lookup, and others)
  must score $`0`$ under both the strict verifier and the progress
  scorer, $`\operatorname{ver}_{W}(s^{(k)})=\Phi_{W}(s^{(k)})=0`$.
- •
  Role independence. Reference solver, alternative solver, strict
  verifier, and progress scorer have disjoint, audited code closures;
  none imports another.
- •
  Solver agreement. The reference solver $`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$, whose
  output scores exactly $`1.0`$,
  $`\operatorname{ver}_{W}(\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(s_{0}))=\Phi_{W}(\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(s_{0}))=1`$,
  and the independently authored alternative solver
  $`\pi^{\mathrm{alt}}`$ must produce identical output trees on every
  seed, $`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}(s_{0})=\pi^{\mathrm{alt}}(s_{0})`$; a
  disagreement is treated as an unresolved specification and the
  environment is rejected.
- •
  Progress validity. The potential $`\Phi_{W}`$ satisfies
  $`0<\Phi_{W}(s)<1`$ on partially correct states, is monotone to
  $`1.0`$ along the reference trajectory, and produces negative
  increments on regression.
- •
  Metamorphic invariance. Semantics-preserving transforms of a correct
  output do not change the grade; stale-output counterfactuals fail.
- •
  Six-seed diversity. At least six seeds yield distinct world
  identities, public-tree hashes, and task ids.
- •
  Public-contract hygiene. No private authority, reference output, or
  verifier code appears in the prompt $`x`$ or the public tree
  $`s_{0}`$.

Why we gate at generation time. The solver audit is independent of the
verifier: neither $`\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}`$ nor $`\pi^{\mathrm{alt}}`$
imports verifier code, so their agreement is evidence about the
specification rather than about the grader. Unlike pipelines that skip
validation and rely on downstream RL to soft-filter unsolvable
tasks ([Ivison et al., 2026](#bib.bib29)), we gate at generation time,
because a dense progress signal is only useful if it is causal and
resists gaming. Procedural seeding also makes decontamination
structural: no two environments share a public tree,
$`s_{0}(E)\neq s_{0}(E^{\prime})`$ for $`E\neq E^{\prime}`$, and none
shares one with an evaluation instance
(Appendix [A.9](#A1.SS9 "A.9 Decontamination ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Gate | What it enforces | Failure class it targets |
| Reward causality | each reward component drops when its cause is mutated | reward credits the wrong outcome |
| Exploit resistance | all 14 gaming probes score $`0`$ | verifier loophole, shortcut acceptance |
| Role independence | disjoint reference/alt/verifier/progress closures | shared bias between solver and grader |
| Solver agreement | reference and alternative solvers yield identical trees | verifier-only acceptance, unresolved spec. |
| Progress validity | $`0<\Phi<1`$; monotone; decreases on regression | invalid process signal |
| Metamorphic invariance | meaning-preserving rewrites keep the grade; stale outputs fail | over-narrow verifier; stale-state acceptance |
| Six-seed diversity | distinct world / public-tree / task id ($`\geq 6`$) | degenerate environment, template repetition |
| Public-contract hygiene | no private authority in the prompt or public tree | answer leakage |

Table 7: Intrinsic qualification gates. Every task environment must pass
all gates; each gate is benchmark-independent, enforced while each
generator is authored, and checked automatically when each environment
is generated. The third column names the failure class each gate is
matched to; passing one gate is not taken as evidence for a property it
does not observe.

### A.7 Dataset Statistics

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | Per-environment horizon (median) |  |  |  | Difficulty (P@1) |  |
| Category | Task envs. | Stages | Input files | Corruptions | Formats | Base 27B | Frontier |
| Math & Science | 264 | 6 | 8 | 1 | 7 | 0.04 | 0.31 |
| ML | 242 | 6 | 12 | 1 | 11 | 0.03 | 0.24 |
| Coding & System | 664 | 6 | 14 | 3 | 19 | 0.03 | 0.30 |
| Tev-Gym (total) | 1,170 | 6 | 12 | 2 | 15 | 0.03 | 0.29 |

Table 8: Composition of Tev-Gym by capability category. Counts are task
environments; horizon columns are per-environment medians over the
category (workflow stages, input files the agent sees, injected
corruptions); “formats” counts distinct wire formats among the inputs.
Base P@1 is the single-rollout pass rate of untrained Qwen3.8-27B under
the training harness, from one probe on our environments; frontier P@1
is the fixed evaluator of
Appendix [A.8](#A1.SS8 "A.8 Comparison Protocol ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
on its 92-environment subsample.

Balance. We quantify balance with the normalized-entropy score
$`\mathrm{Balance}=\exp(H)/K`$, where
$`H=-\sum_{i=1}^{K}p_{i}\log p_{i}`$ and $`p_{i}`$ is the fraction of
environments in bucket $`i`$ of $`K`$; the score lies in $`[1/K,1]`$
with $`1`$ perfectly uniform. Tev-Gym attains 0.89 across the three
capability categories and 0.91 across the seven domains. Horizon. An
environment has a median of 6 workflow stages (range 3–12), 8 progress
nodes, 6 reward components, and 2 injected corruptions (1–7), and
exposes to the agent a median of 12 input files (up to 501) totaling
51 KB (up to 6.1 MB) in 23 wire formats (32 file extensions), described
by a task prompt of 583 tokens at the median (Qwen tokenizer; maximum
944). Under the training harness of
§[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
an untrained Qwen3.8-27B uses a median of 19 tool calls and generates
30k tokens per rollout, and 52% of its rollouts exhaust the 65,536-token
context before submitting.

Difficulty. The same rollouts give the base-model difficulty: across our
single-rollout probes (one per environment), 3.2% of environments are
fully solved (mean graded score 0.06). Terminal reward alone is
therefore near zero on most of the corpus, which is the regime
§[3](#S3 "3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
addresses. Under the fixed evaluator of
Appendix [A.8](#A1.SS8 "A.8 Comparison Protocol ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
Gemini 3.6 Flash reaches pass@1 29% and pass@8 40% on a fixed
92-environment subsample
(Table [1](#S1.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
so the open–frontier gap is roughly $`3\%`$ against $`29\%`$; on 26 of
those 92 environments, and on 52 further environments probed at the
small and full scale tiers, it solves nothing and submits an answer in
at most 29% of its rollouts, so a substantial part of Tev-Gym is beyond
current frontier reach as authored.

### A.8 Comparison Protocol

Agent time. “Agent time” in
Table [1](#S1.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
is measured rather than estimated. The fixed evaluator of the difficulty
protocol below, Gemini 3.6 Flash, is run on a fixed 100-task subsample
of each corpus, and for every rollout we record the wall-clock time from
the agent’s first action to its last step. The interval includes every
model call and retry and every command execution in between; it excludes
only sandbox setup before the first action and the separate test run
after the last step (Tev-Gym’s host-side grade at submission, 0.3 s at
the median, falls inside the last step). The table reports the median
and mean over rollouts, in minutes. For the prior corpora these are the
rollouts of the difficulty measurement below (Harbor terminus-2, 60
turns, 8 rollouts per task, timed by Harbor’s agent_execution interval).
That budget ends only 1–13% of their rollouts, and every rollout it ends
has already run longer than its corpus’s median, so the reported
prior-corpus medians equal those of an unlimited budget (only the means
could rise). Tev-Gym’s rollouts run longer (28% use more than 60 tool
calls), so for Tev-Gym we lift the budget until it no longer binds: we
draw a fixed 100-environment subsample from all 1,170 environments (seed
20260915, stratified by domain 9.1–9.7) and run the evaluator in the
single-bash harness of
§[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
with a budget of 300 tool calls and 2 h of agent wall-clock per rollout,
2 rollouts per environment. No rollout reaches either limit (median 41
tool calls, maximum 145), so the values are not truncated: median
12.4 min and mean 14.8 min, against a median of 0.5–2.0 min for the
prior corpora. The measure compares corpora under one evaluator and is
not an estimate of human effort. Terminal-Universe releases no
environments and cannot be timed. “Progressive reward” marks a training
signal defined on intermediate workspace states during an episode
(§[3](#S3 "3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"));
SETA’s test fraction is computed once at the end of the episode and
therefore does not qualify.

Difficulty. We measure difficulty uniformly: a single evaluator,
Gemini 3.6 Flash, is run on a fixed subsample of every corpus at an
identical budget (60 tool calls, 8 rollouts per task, temperature 1.0),
and we report pass@1 (mean over rollouts) and the unbiased pass@8 (macro
over tasks). For Tev-Gym the evaluator runs in the single-bash harness
of
§[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
on a fixed 92-environment subsample, reaching pass@1 29% and pass@8 40%;
it solves nothing on 26 of the 92, nor on the 52 further environments
probed at the small and full tiers
(Appendix [A.7](#A1.SS7 "A.7 Dataset Statistics ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")).
For the prior corpora we run the same evaluator under the official
Harbor terminus-2 agent on a fixed 100-task subsample of each (seed
20260915, stratified by the corpus’s own domain or repository field
where present): SETA 80/88 (camel-ai/seta-env-release, CC BY-SA 4.0;
100/100 tasks), TMAX-15K 74/80 (pre-converted Harbor release of
allenai/TMax-15K, ODC-BY; 100/100), Endless Terminals 83/89
(obiwan96/endless-terminals, MIT; 99/100), SWE-Smith 47/52
(SWE-bench/SWE-smith, MIT; 100/100), and CLI-Gym 69/74
(LiberCoders/CLI-Gym, MIT; 89/100). Excluded are tasks the Docker
sandbox cannot faithfully build: 11 CLI-Gym and 1 Endless tasks whose
released Dockerfiles reference build-context files absent from the
release or perform build-time /etc/hosts or /proc writes (these are
CLI-Gym’s hardest systems-level corruptions, so its number is a mild
upper bound); one TMAX task is under-sampled. Every exclusion is
recorded. Terminal-Universe is a task-generation method whose 32k
environments are not publicly released, so its difficulty could not be
measured and its cells are marked NA. The evaluator and budget are
shared across all rows; only the scaffold differs (prior corpora
terminus-2, Tev-Gym single-bash). Sizes are as reported by each corpus;
“oracle” names what grades a task and “grading metric” what it emits to
a learner, and a corpus is credited with a progressive reward only if it
emits more than a terminal scalar.

Table [9](#A1.T9 "Table 9 ‣ A.8 Comparison Protocol ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
complements the size and difficulty comparison with the axes on which
generation pipelines differ in design: what anchors generation, whether
a model is in the loop, what admits a task and when, whether difficulty
and coverage are controlled or only reported, and what a learner
receives short of a terminal pass.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Corpus | Anchor | Model in generation | Validation | Difficulty control | Coverage control | Progress signal |
| SWE-Smith ([Yang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib31)) | repository | bug injection, task text | tests run post hoc | – | – | none |
| Terminal-Universe ([Wu et al., 2026](#bib.bib30)) | agent trajectory | trajectory, task text | pytest post hoc | – | – | none |
| TMAX-15K ([Ivison et al., 2026](#bib.bib29)) | task text | task, container, tests | none; RL soft-filters | – | topic balancing | none |
| SETA ([Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)) | task text | task, tests | solvability post hoc | – | – | test fraction |
| Endless Terminals ([Gandhi et al., 2026](#bib.bib40)) | task text | task, container, tests | solvability post hoc | – | – | none |
| CLI-Gym ([Lin et al., 2026](#bib.bib41)) | agent trajectory | trajectory, task text | tests post hoc | – | – | none |
| Tev-Gym (ours) | latent world | generator code (once, reviewed) | 8 gates at generation | scale $`\times`$ DAG depth | strength-2 covering array | staged $`\Phi`$ |

Table 9: How recent agentic corpora are generated and gated. Axes follow
the ACE lens ([Zeng et al., 2026a](#bib.bib66)). *Anchor* is the factor
from which the others are derived; *model in generation* lists what a
language model writes; *validation* is what admits a task and when;
*difficulty* and *coverage* record whether the corpus controls them (“–”
marks no explicit control reported); *progress signal* is what a learner
receives short of a terminal pass. Prior-corpus cells are transcribed
from the cited papers.

### A.9 Decontamination

Structural cleanliness. Because each task is projected from a freshly
seeded procedural world, no two environments, and in particular no
environment and no evaluation instance, share a public tree; the
six-seed diversity gate enforces this by hash. Tev-Gym therefore
contains no verbatim copy of any evaluation task.

Measured overlap. We additionally measure lexical overlap between the
task prompt of every one of the 1,170 environments and our evaluation
sets with a 13-gram sliding window over lower-cased alphanumeric tokens,
following standard contamination protocol. The evaluation texts are the
task cards of the 18 computing-and-math tasks (and, as a superset, all
23 tasks of that split; 9,010 distinct 13-grams), the instructions of
the 74 TB 3.0/4.0 tasks (21,138) and of the 89 TB 2.1 tasks (13,292). No
environment shares a single 13-gram with any evaluation task (0 of 1,170
against each set; maximum shared fraction 0.0%), and none shares an
8-gram; overlap appears only at 5-grams, in generic phrasing such as
“not modify files under input”. A control confirms that the comparator
is sensitive: the two TB sets share 156 13-grams with each other.

Per-set breakdown.
Table [10](#A1.T10 "Table 10 ‣ A.9 Decontamination ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
gives the per-set view behind these statements, with TB 3.0 and TB 4.0
separated (full sets and the CM subsets of
Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))
and TB 2.1 included, and adds three statistics to the 13-gram count.
*8-gram hits* repeats the count at the shorter window; *max token
coverage* is, for the worst evaluation task, the fraction of its tokens
that fall inside an 8-gram also present in some environment prompt; *NN
Jaccard* is each evaluation task’s largest token-set Jaccard similarity
to any of the 1,170 prompts, summarized by its median and maximum over
tasks. The calibration block applies the same code to a task scored
against the other tasks of its own benchmark, which shares a 13-gram in
every TB case (the common closing instruction) and reaches a
nearest-neighbor Jaccard of $`0.37`$. Against that scale, no evaluation
task shares an 8-gram with any environment, and nearest-neighbor
similarity to the whole dataset stays at $`0.13`$ (median) and $`0.18`$
(maximum) on TB 3.0 and 4.0 and $`0.10`$ and $`0.16`$ on TB 2.1; on
ALE-CM it is $`0.22`$ and $`0.38`$.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Evaluation set | Tasks | 13-gram hits | 8-gram hits | Max token cov. | NN Jaccard med. / max |
| ALE-CM | 18 | 0 | 0 | 0.0% | 0.22 / 0.38 |
| TB 3.0, all | 74 | 0 | 0 | 0.0% | 0.13 / 0.18 |
| TB 3.0, CM subset | 22 | 0 | 0 | 0.0% | 0.14 / 0.18 |
| TB 4.0, all | 66 | 0 | 0 | 0.0% | 0.13 / 0.18 |
| TB 4.0, CM subset | 18 | 0 | 0 | 0.0% | 0.14 / 0.18 |
| TB 2.1 | 89 | 0 | 0 | 0.0% | 0.10 / 0.16 |
| Calibration: same code, same-benchmark tasks |  |  |  |  |  |
| ALE-CM task vs. other ALE-CM tasks | 18 | 2 | 13 | 26.0% | 0.28 / 0.34 |
| TB 3.0 CM task vs. other TB 3.0 tasks | 22 | 22 | 22 | 42.5% | 0.24 / 0.37 |
| TB 4.0 CM task vs. other TB 4.0 tasks | 18 | 18 | 18 | 42.5% | 0.25 / 0.37 |

Table 10: Lexical overlap between all 1,170 environments of Tev-Gym and
each evaluation set. *Hits*: evaluation tasks sharing at least one
13-gram (resp. 8-gram) of lower-cased alphanumeric tokens with any
environment prompt. *Max token cov.*: for the worst evaluation task, the
share of its tokens covered by 8-grams that also occur in some
environment prompt. *NN Jaccard*: per evaluation task, the largest
token-set Jaccard similarity to any environment prompt; median / max
over tasks. CM = the 22- / 18-task computing-and-math subsets evaluated
in
Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").
The calibration block applies the same code to same-benchmark,
different-task pairs.

### A.10 Three Worked Training Environments

Two generalizations of
§[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
are easiest to see on concrete environments. First, the public tree
$`s_{0}`$ need not be text: Example 1, a cloud-operations task from one
of Tev-Gym’s seven domains, stages a billing-dashboard screenshot whose
three findings appear in no CSV. Second, nothing in the pipeline is
specific to the seven computing-and-math domains: Examples 2 and 3 were
synthesized by the same pipeline for two other domains of Agents’ Last
Exam ([Sun et al., 2026](#bib.bib65)), energy engineering (closed-loop
building control) and accounting (financial-statement reconstruction).
For each we present the training environment only, in two parts: the
*public view*, the prompt $`x`$ and the input tree $`s_{0}`$ that are
all the agent ever receives, and the *ground truth* sealed behind them,
the latent world $`W`$, the reference deliverables derived from it, and
what the grader compares. The three also span the two ways a latent
world grounds a reference: in Examples 1 and 3 the world is a generated
record set and the reference follows from it by construction; in
Example 2 the world is a pinned simulator plus a declared procedure, so
the reference is a *tool oracle*, produced by running that procedure
once inside the sealed image. Paths are relative to the agent’s working
directory, the agent writes its deliverables to output/, and all three
run in a pinned container with the network disabled.

#### Example 1: multimodal cloud cost optimization (Cloud Operations)

Public view. The container provides python3 and pandas. The agent
receives the prompt below and three inputs: an inventory CSV of 430 EC2,
RDS, and S3 resources with utilization percentiles, a pricing table, and
a rendered billing-dashboard screenshot
(Figure [6](#A1.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Example 1: multimodal cloud cost optimization (Cloud Operations) ‣ A.10 Three Worked Training Environments ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))
whose lower panel lists three findings, an idle NAT gateway, an
unattached Elastic IP, and a stale CloudWatch log group, that appear in
no CSV. This is an extra-large instance of its generator.

\# AWS cost optimization analysis (430 inventory resources)

You are an AWS cost optimization analyst working on Linux. Three inputs
are staged under ‘input/‘:

- ‘input/aws_billing_dashboard.png‘ - a synthetic AWS billing dashboard
screenshot. It shows a per-service cost breakdown AND three
’dashboard-only’ findings (an idle NAT gateway, an unattached Elastic
IP, and a stale CloudWatch log group) that are NOT present in the usage
CSV. Inspect the image directly to recover them.

- ‘input/resource_usage.csv‘ - the EC2/RDS/S3 inventory with columns
‘resource_id,resource_type,name,state,instance_type,cpu_p95,mem_p95,connections_p95,size_gb,ia_access_ratio‘.

- ‘input/aws_pricing_reference.csv‘ - the deterministic pricing table
(‘sku,dimension,unit,price_usd‘).

Apply these rules and price every saving from the pricing table:

- Downsize a running EC2 instance one tier when ‘cpu_p95 \< 20‘ and
‘mem_p95 \< 50‘.

- Downsize an available RDS instance one tier when ‘cpu_p95 \< 25‘ and
‘connections_p95 \< 10‘.

- Move an S3 bucket to Infrequent Access when ‘ia_access_ratio \< 0.10‘
and ‘size_gb \>= 100‘.

- Include the three dashboard-only findings (delete the idle NAT
gateway, release the unattached Elastic IP, delete the stale CloudWatch
log group).

Write ‘output/optimization_report.json‘ with an ‘executive_summary‘
object and a ‘recommendations‘ array, and ‘output/savings_summary.csv‘
with columns
‘resource_id,resource_type,name,action,current_cost,projected_cost,monthly_savings‘.

Scoring is weighted: 30% coverage F1 over the hidden recommendation set
(including the three dashboard-only resources), 30% action correctness,
25% savings accuracy (full credit within +/-20% relative error), and 15%
report completeness.

\# input/resource_usage.csv (6 of 430 rows)

resource_id,resource_type,name,state,instance_type,cpu_p95,mem_p95,connections_p95,size_gb,ia_access_ratio

db-000067,rds,rds-svc-067,available,db.m5.xlarge,62.12,,346,,

db-000048,rds,rds-svc-048,available,db.m5.xlarge,18.48,,8,,

i-000161,ec2,ec2-app-161,running,m5.2xlarge,45.55,74.81,,,

i-000000,ec2,ec2-app-000,running,m5.2xlarge,11.97,41.34,,,

s3-000015,s3,data-bucket-015,,,,,,5923,0.052

s3-000118,s3,data-bucket-118,,,,,,19,0.596

Ground truth. The latent world $`W`$ is the generator’s record of the
fleet: the 430 inventory rows with their utilization percentiles and
sizes, the pricing tiers in their declared order, and three planted
dashboard-only findings with their dashboard-reported costs. Rendering
splits $`W`$ across three formats: the inventory becomes
resource_usage.csv, the tiers become aws_pricing_reference.csv, and the
three findings are drawn only into the PNG, next to a per-service cost
table that is context, so that no text file contains them. The reference
follows from $`W`$ by applying the prompt’s four rules with the pricing
table: 730 hours per month, one tier down along the declared order, S3
Standard to Infrequent Access per GB-month, and recommendations sorted
by savings descending, then by id. Each recommendation’s current cost,
projected cost, and savings are computed from unrounded prices and
rounded to cents separately, so a row’s savings can differ by one cent
from the difference of its two rounded costs (s3-000126, 7,734 GB:
$`177.88-96.68=81.20`$, savings 81.21). Of the six rows above, three
qualify: db-000048 (CPU 18.48, 8 connections) drops to db.m5.large and
saves 124.83; i-000000 (CPU 11.97, memory 41.34) drops to m5.xlarge and
saves 140.16; s3-000015 (5,923 GB at 5.2% infrequent access) moves to
Infrequent Access and saves 62.19. For this seed the rules yield 148
recommendations, 74 EC2 downsizes, 27 RDS downsizes, 44 S3 tier moves,
and the three dashboard findings. The executive summary sums the rounded
rows column by column: current cost 32,629.73 USD, projected cost
16,234.79, and monthly savings 16,395.02. The savings total therefore
exceeds current minus projected (16,394.94) by 0.08, the sum of the
one-cent residues on 12 S3 rows, far inside the grader’s 20% savings
tolerance. The grader applies two hard gates (both files parse and are
non-empty; total savings lie between zero and total current cost), then
scores the weighted rubric the prompt announces: coverage F1 of
recommended ids against the hidden set (30%), action correctness on
matched ids (30%), savings accuracy with full credit within 20% relative
error and linear decay to zero at 100% (25%), and completeness of the
summary fields, per-recommendation columns, and CSV header (15%); it
also reports recall of the three dashboard-only ids separately,
accepting the CloudWatch id as printed on the dashboard as an alias of
the canonical id.

#### Example 2: closed-loop building MPC (Energy, Power & Nuclear Engineering)

Public view. The container is the engineering-energyplus-mpc profile
(EnergyPlus 22.1.0, cvxpy 1.6.5, numpy 2.1.3; 4 cores, 8 GB, 5 h wall
clock). The agent receives the prompt below and the input tree that
follows it, and nothing else. This is a medium instance of its
generator.

\# EnergyPlus closed-loop building MPC (7 days at 15 minutes)

Use the exact toolchain declared in ‘input/instance.json‘. Inspect
‘input/model.idf‘, ‘input/weather.epw‘, ‘input/experiment_plan.json‘,
and ‘input/output_contract.json‘. The model is the pinned EnergyPlus
22.1 one-zone example with a declared internal-mass transformation and a
schedule actuator driving the ‘MPC Cooling Sink‘.

Run the deterministic thermostat baseline and supplied excitation at
15-minute resolution. Fit the declared second-order input-output thermal
model over both EnergyPlus traces and preserve its training and held-out
diagnostics. This is a 3R2C-comparable dynamic-order model, not a claim
that physical resistance and capacitance parameters were identified.

Implement two distinct cvxpy/OSQP receding-horizon policies using the
declared price, peak-load, comfort, and switching weights. Deploy each
through the EnergyPlus Python API: at every zone timestep read the
simulated zone temperature, solve the policy, and actuate the first
control. A precomputed open-loop schedule does not satisfy this task.

Write the nine artifacts named in ‘input/output_contract.json‘. The
three data traces cover the complete simulation window. The two action
files and comparison metrics cover the final 24 hours. RC logs must
expose model coefficients, training and held-out calibration,
policy-specific closed-loop error, objective weights, and solver status
counts.

The grader densely compares all nine artifacts to a reference generated
and rerun with EnergyPlus 22.1 and cvxpy in the pinned profile.

"energy_saving": "mpc_energy_saving_data.csv",

"demand_response": "mpc_demand_response_data.csv"},

"trace_columns": \["step", "hour", "cooling_w", "t_zone", "setpoint",
"outdoor_temp_c",

"occupancy", "cooling_fraction", "electric_kw", "price_per_kwh",

"comfort_min_c", "comfort_max_c", "one_step_predicted_zone_temp_c"\],

"action_files": \["mpc_actions_energy_saving.csv",
"mpc_actions_demand_response.csv"\],

"rc_log_files": \["rc_log_energy_saving.json",
"rc_log_demand_response.json"\],

"metrics_file": "metrics_comparison.csv",

"metrics_rows": \["baseline", "mpc_energy_saving",
"mpc_demand_response"\],

"metrics_columns": \["cooling_kwh", "elec_kwh", "cost_usd",
"peak_load_kw",

"peak_hour_avg_kw", "discomfort_dh", "calibration_rmse_c"\],

"summary_file": "results_summary.json",

"numeric_rounding": {"csv": 8, "model_coefficients": 10, "summary": 8}

}

Ground truth. The latent world is the pinned simulator together with the
declared procedure: the EnergyPlus model, the weather file, and the
schedules, weights, and identification recipe of instance.json fix a
unique correct value for every field of the nine deliverables. The
reference is therefore produced by executing that procedure once, inside
the same pinned image, by the sealed reference builder, which the agent
never sees. The builder (i) checks the SHA-256 of the shipped
1ZoneUncontrolled.idf and Golden TMY3 weather file against the values
pinned in instance.json and applies the declared edits to the model (run
period 1–7 July, four timesteps per hour, an InternalMass of 48 m², and
a Schedule:Constant actuator driving a $`2.0406`$ kW OtherEquipment
cooling sink); (ii) runs EnergyPlus twice through its Python API, once
under the two-position thermostat (declared setpoint schedule, deadband
0.5 ^(∘)C, on-fraction 0.85) and once under the prescribed 0.9/0.1
excitation schedule, reading the zone temperature and writing the
actuator at every zone timestep; (iii) fits the declared second-order
input–output model

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
T[t]=a_{1}T[t-1]+a_{2}T[t-2]+b_{0}T_{\mathrm{out}}[t]+b_{1}T_{\mathrm{out}}[t-1]+c_{0}u[t]+c_{1}u[t-1]+d
``` |  |

by ridge least squares ($`\lambda=10^{-8}`$) on the first 80% of each
trace with the last 20% held out, and asserts a negative cooling gain,
$`c_{0}+c_{1}<0`$; (iv) runs EnergyPlus twice more in closed loop,
solving at every zone timestep a 24-step cvxpy/OSQP receding-horizon
problem with the policy’s declared weights and actuating its first
control; and (v) writes the nine artifacts with the declared rounding:
three 672-row traces, two 96-row action files over the final 24 hours,
two logs exposing the seven coefficients, training and held-out RMSE,
closed-loop one-step RMSE, objective weights, and solver-status counts,
the $`3\times 7`$ metrics table, and the summary. A round-trip
self-check re-fits the model and re-reads every artifact the way the
grader will (coefficients within $`10^{-7}`$, row counts, distinct
policy weights) before the environment is published. The grader then
compares the agent’s nine files with these references cell by cell (CSV)
and leaf by leaf (JSON) at a tolerance of $`10^{-4}`$, scoring each
artifact by its fraction of matching fields. Every artifact is a
function of the closed-loop trajectories, so a precomputed open-loop
schedule, a first-order model, or a single policy cannot reproduce them.

#### Example 3: financial-statement reconstruction (Accounting & Finance)

Public view. The container is the finance-foss-headless profile
(Python 3.10 only; 4 cores, 4 GB, 30 min wall clock). The agent receives
the prompt below, a policy document, and a filing packet for a
fictitious company, Juniper Ridge Holdings plc, in the two shapes an
analyst meets in practice: SEC “companyfacts” JSON and inline-XBRL HTML
filings. This is a medium instance of its generator.

\# Reconstruct and reconcile annual financial statements

Reconstruct the annual statements from the multi-document filing packet
under ‘input/filings/‘ and the SEC-shaped ‘input/companyfacts.json‘. Use
‘input/filing_index.json‘, ‘input/account_mapping.csv‘, and the
authority and fallback rules in ‘input/reconstruction_policy.md‘.

Write three deliverables:

- ‘output/financial_statements.json‘, with company metadata, USD-million
unit metadata, and a ‘periods‘ array containing balance sheet, income
statement, and cash-flow sections.

- ‘output/evidence.csv‘, with the exact columns ‘period, statement,
line_item, selected_value, concept, source_file, locator, citation,
authority_basis‘, in period and mapping order. Every locator must
resolve to the selected source value.

- ‘output/exceptions.json‘, listing every cross-source conflict,
explicit amendment restatement, accepted taxonomy alias, and
missing-structured-fact fallback with its policy-based resolution.

Preserve integer USD millions. Verify the accounting, income, and cash
roll-forward identities for every period before writing the files. Do
not use quarterly, unaudited, pro forma, or wrong-unit evidence.

\# Annual statement reconstruction policy

All output values are USD millions. Companyfacts ‘USD‘ values are whole
USD and must be divided by 1,000,000. Use exact fiscal-year and
period-end matches, annual ‘FY‘ facts, and Forms 10-K or 10-K/A only.
Ignore quarterly, non-USD, pro forma, and unaudited supplemental facts.

Within Companyfacts, choose the latest ‘filed‘ date and then accession
among all accepted concepts in ‘account_mapping.csv‘. Within filing
documents, a 10-K/A overrides only the rows it explicitly restates;
otherwise retain the original 10-K row. When the latest structured fact
and latest filing row disagree, the structured Companyfacts value
controls. If no accepted structured fact exists, use the latest
applicable filing row. Record every cross-source conflict, amendment
restatement, taxonomy alias, and structured-fact fallback in
‘exceptions.json‘. Every evidence row must cite an exact JSON pointer or
HTML fragment that resolves to the selected value.

The excerpts below follow one line item, cash and cash equivalents for
FY2025, through the packet: under its balance-sheet concept it is
reported five times with four different values.

Ground truth. The latent world $`W`$ is the company’s true two-period
statements: 16 integer line items in USD millions for fiscal years 2025
and 2024, each ending December 31. $`W`$ satisfies the three identities
the prompt asks the agent to verify, assets $`=`$ liabilities $`+`$
equity, net income $`=`$ revenue $`-`$ cost of revenue $`-`$ operating
expenses, and ending cash $`=`$ beginning cash $`+`$ operating $`+`$
investing $`+`$ financing cash flow, as well as the ties that connect
real statements: the four asset lines sum to total assets (FY2025:
$`246+147+62+732=1{,}187`$), balance-sheet cash equals cash-flow ending
cash in each year (246 and 256), and FY2025 beginning cash equals FY2024
ending cash (256). Rendering projects $`W`$ into the two public formats:
each value becomes a whole-dollar structured fact under its us-gaap
concept in companyfacts.json and an inline-XBRL row, in millions and
with a stable fragment id, in the fiscal year’s 10-K. The defect plan
then corrupts the clean packet, but only in sources that the policy
rejects. The original FY2025 10-K misstates three balance-sheet lines,
in its rows and in its structured facts alike (cash 239, accounts
receivable 138, other current assets 726, against 246, 147, and 732 in
$`W`$); a 10-K/A restates one row (cash, 239 $`\to`$ 243), and the
restated value is still wrong; the whole FY2025 fact set is refiled in
companyfacts.json under the 10-K/A accession with the true values, so
the structured feed and the amendment disagree about the same
restatement; an 8-K reports the same concepts under quarterly Q32025
contexts; the filings carry an “unaudited supplemental data” section;
outflows are printed in accounting parentheses, (110); and each line
item admits a second, company-extension concept (jrh:) that this seed
leaves unused. Because the defect plan is declared, the correct
resolution of each of these is known without running a solver, and the
three reference deliverables follow from $`W`$, the defect plan, and the
policy text. Since every defect lies in a rejected source, the values
the policy selects are those of $`W`$, and financial_statements.json is
$`W`$ itself. The rejected values are also the ones that break the ties:
the original 10-K’s asset lines sum to 1,165 against its total assets of
1,187, and neither 239 nor 243 matches the ending cash of 246.
exceptions.json lists exactly the four conflicts the defect plan
created, each with the resolution the policy dictates; the amendment
entry records the filing-level choice (the 10-K/A row, 243, supersedes
the original, 239), while its selected value is the structured 246 that
overrides both. evidence.csv holds one row per line item and period, 32
in all, whose locator is the JSON pointer of the latest annual fact and
whose authority basis names the rule that selected it. A round-trip
self-check reselects every value from the public packet, verifies the
three identities and the three ties above in every period, and asserts
that the observed exception set equals the expected one before the
environment is published. The grader compares the agent’s three files
with these references leaf by leaf and cell by cell at zero tolerance.

## Appendix B Dense Reward Details

This appendix expands
§[3](#S3 "3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

Wasted groups under terminal reward. The all-fail fraction $`(1-p)^{G}`$
of
§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Terminal Reward Starves Group-Relative RL ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
stays high even for larger groups, still $`44\%`$ at $`p{=}0.05`$ with
$`G{=}16`$, and even on synthesized terminal tasks [Shen et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib23) find that only about half of all tasks yield any
reward variance under binary reward. The same starvation afflicts
actor-critic methods such as PPO ([Schulman et al., 2017](#bib.bib4)) in
a softer form, since a critic fit to near-constant returns yields
near-zero advantages everywhere; GRPO merely makes it exact.

Relation to policy-invariant shaping. PPRS
(Eq. ([4](#S3.E4 "Equation 4 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")))
is not policy-invariant shaping in the sense of [Ng et al.
(1999)](#bib.bib5). Classical shaping zeroes the potential of the
terminal state, so the shaping terms sum to the same constant
$`-\Phi(s_{0})=0`$ on every trajectory and leave the objective
unchanged. We keep the terminal potential, so the shaped return is
terminal success plus $`\lambda\,\Phi(s_{T})`$ and defines a graded
objective; zeroing it would give every all-fail group identical rewards
again and reinstate the starvation of
§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Terminal Reward Starves Group-Relative RL ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
which is why the term is kept. What is preserved is the ranking of
trajectories: a passing trajectory earns $`1+\lambda`$ and a failing one
at most $`\lambda`$, so a deterministic policy that maximizes one
objective maximizes the other, and no trajectory is rewarded for trading
completion against partial progress. What is not preserved is the
ranking of stochastic policies in expectation. Let $`\pi_{1}`$ and
$`\pi_{2}`$ pass with probabilities $`p_{1}<p_{2}`$ and let
$`\bar{\Phi}_{k}\in[0,1]`$ be the mean final potential of their failing
rollouts. Then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}[R]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2}}[R]=(p_{1}-p_{2})(1+\lambda)+\lambda\bigl[(1-p_{1})\bar{\Phi}_{1}-(1-p_{2})\bar{\Phi}_{2}\bigr]\;\leq\;(p_{1}-p_{2})(1+\lambda)+\lambda(1-p_{1}),
``` |  |

so the shaped objective can prefer the less successful policy only if
$`p_{2}-p_{1}<\lambda(1-p_{1})/(1+\lambda)\leq\lambda/(1+\lambda)`$,
which is $`0.2`$ at $`\lambda=0.25`$, and only when its failures end
close to complete while the other’s end near empty. Every result we
report is a terminal pass rate under the strict verifier, so any such
distortion would appear directly in the tables; the sparse arm of
Table [5](#S4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ 4.3 Ablation Study ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
trained on the same tasks with the terminal reward alone, is the
control. $`\lambda`$ was fixed at $`0.25`$ in every run and not tuned.

Example progress contract. For the warehouse environment of
Figure [3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
the nodes are readable, schema, dimensions, facts, normalization,
relations, aggregates, quality_report, and summary; relations, for
instance, requires dimensions and facts. The partial scorer $`c_{v}`$
uses set precision and recall for record-valued outputs and declared
tolerances for numeric ones, and never re-runs the strict verifier. Each
node’s credit is capped by the weakest of its prerequisites
(§[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 A Progress Potential from the Latent World ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")),
so a correct aggregate built on a wrong fact table earns nothing.

Measured groups. To see the starvation of
§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Terminal Reward Starves Group-Relative RL ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
on real rollouts, we graded the workspace after every turn for $`G{=}8`$
rollouts on each of 12 seeds of the warehouse environment, for the
untrained Qwen3.8-27B and for the trained 27B checkpoint, under the
training harness (65k-token context, 60 tool calls), which grades the
terminal reward only when the policy issues the submit command. Terminal
reward is $`0`$ on all 192 rollouts, so every one of the 24 groups has
zero advantage under
Eq. ([2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 3.1 Terminal Reward Starves Group-Relative RL ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"));
yet under $`\Phi`$ every group has non-zero variance, with rollouts
ending on the prerequisite-gated plateaus $`0.05`$ (readable and schema
partially present), $`0.26`$ (every table partially loaded), $`0.69`$
(schema and facts complete), $`0.78`$, $`0.90`$, and $`1`$.
Figure [4](#S2.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 2.2 Coverage, Calibration, and Qualification ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
shows one of these groups.

Shaping within a failing group. In a group in which no rollout passes,
$`\lambda`$ cancels and the potential *is* the objective; [Wang
(2026)](#bib.bib12) shows that GRPO agents collapse when this happens
with a shaping term that is not aligned with the task, and it is safe
here only because $`\Phi`$ is exact progress toward the ground-truth
world. Among failing rollouts, the shaped reward therefore imposes a
total order by how much of the workflow they completed, and it penalizes
regressions, such as overwriting a correct table with a corrupted one,
through negative increments $`r_{t}<0`$. The fraction of groups that
yield gradient rises from $`1-(1-p)^{G}`$ to the fraction whose rollouts
are not all stalled at the same stage. In training, $`R(\tau)`$ enters
with per-turn credit
(Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")):
turn $`t`$ receives the increment $`\lambda r_{t}`$ and the final turn
additionally receives the terminal term.

Relation to potential-based shaping. In the language of potential-based
shaping ([Ng et al., 1999](#bib.bib5)), $`\Phi`$ is the potential and
$`r_{t}`$ its difference. Classical shaping requires that the shaped
objective have the same optimal policies as the original; we
deliberately do not, because the whole point is that the shaped
objective is *not* indifferent to partial progress. What we retain is
agreement on maximizers (any passing rollout outscores any failing one)
and anchoring ($`\Phi=1`$ exactly when the verifier passes), so the
acceptance set of a task is unchanged. This is the opposite route from
the usual remedy for tasks beyond a learner’s frontier, which is to
simplify, scaffold, or defer the task until some rollout
succeeds ([Florensa et al., 2018](#bib.bib76); [Chen et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib73); [Wolf et al., 2026](#bib.bib75); [Zeng et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib66)); we leave the task and the harness fixed and raise
the resolution of the reward. We prefer a task-aligned potential to the
test-pass fraction of [Shen et al. (2026)](#bib.bib23) and the
passing-assertion count of [Yang et al. (2026)](#bib.bib24), which score
only what the terminal tests probe and carry no notion of regression.
The signal is also denser *within* a group that contains a success: the
lone passing rollout is contrasted not with an undifferentiated mass of
failures but with a graded spectrum of near-misses. Because $`\Phi`$ is
produced by a scorer with a code closure disjoint from the verifier’s
and has passed the causality and exploit-resistance gates of
Appendix [A.6](#A1.SS6 "A.6 Qualification Gates ‣ Appendix A Data Construction Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
none of the probed gaming strategies applied to the public tree earns
$`\Phi>0`$, and $`\Phi`$ rises only when the output tree matches more of
the workflow’s correct intermediate and final artifacts.

## Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details

### C.1 Training Recipe and Hyperparameters

We optimize with DPPO: group-centered advantages, a total-variation
divergence bound of $`0.1`$ on the policy ratio, and no KL anchor.
Groups whose rewards have zero variance are discarded and refilled by
active sampling, so every step sees 8 informative groups. The reward is
Eq. ([4](#S3.E4 "Equation 4 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))
with $`\lambda=0.25`$, applied with per-turn credit: turn $`t`$ receives
the increment $`\lambda r_{t}`$, the final turn additionally receives
the terminal term, and each turn’s advantage is its return-to-go minus
the group mean of that return at the same turn index.
Table [11](#A3.T11 "Table 11 ‣ C.1 Training Recipe and Hyperparameters ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
lists the recipe shared by every run; runs differ only in the corpus
subset, the reward mechanism, the model size, and the hardware they ran
on.

|  |  |
|----|----|
| Setting |  Value |
| Objective |  group-relative policy gradient with centered advantages (Eq. ([2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 3.1 Terminal Reward Starves Group-Relative RL ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"))) |
| Trust region |  total-variation divergence bound $`0.1`$ on the policy ratio (DPPO loss); no KL anchor ($`\beta{=}0`$) |
| Rollouts per task ($`G`$) |  24 |
| Unique tasks per step |  8 (192 rollouts per step) |
| Active sampling |  zero-variance groups discarded and refilled |
| Optimizer |  AdamW, learning rate $`10^{-6}`$, constant schedule, one epoch per batch |
| Sampling temperature |  1.0 |
| Prompt / response budget |  2,048 prompt tokens; 65,536 response tokens |
| Per-turn budget |  16,384 tokens; at most 64 tool calls per rollout |
| Tools |  a single bash tool in a per-task container (python:3.13-slim base image) |
| Reward |  Eq. ([4](#S3.E4 "Equation 4 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")) with $`\lambda{=}0.25`$; per-turn credit, return-to-go with $`\gamma{=}1`$, per-turn-index group baseline |
| Terminal reward |  strict verifier score at submission, $`1.0`$ for a pass |
| Precision / parallelism |  bf16 with fp32 LM head; ZeRO-3, sequence parallelism 4 (9B) |
| Learner / inference split |  16 learner GPUs + 16 inference engines on 32-GPU runs; 16 + 48 on the 64-GPU 27B run |
| Seed |  42 |

Table 11: Training hyperparameters shared by every RL run in this paper.

### C.2 Evaluation Protocol

ALE-CM consists of 18 expert-authored long-horizon tasks in three
capability categories (Math & Science, 8 tasks; ML, 5; Coding & System,
5), each graded in $`[0,1]`$ by its own strict grader. Every Qwen model,
base or trained, runs in *Vanillux2*, the single-bash harness used in
training, with 64 turns and 16,384 tokens per turn and 5 independent
rollouts per task. Frontier models run in their native agent harnesses
(Codex, Claude Code, Gemini CLI), the setting most favorable to them;
the largest open model, Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B, runs in Vanillux2. We report
the mean graded score (S@1), the fraction of rollouts that fully solve
the task (P@1), and their best-of-5 counterparts (S@5, P@5). On TB 3.0
and 4.0 ([Merrill et al., 2026](#bib.bib39); [Marten et al.,
2026](#bib.bib37); [Marten, 2026](#bib.bib38)) we evaluate the “CM”
subsets (22 and 18 tasks; selection described below); on TB 2.1 (89
tasks) we use the benchmark’s reference agent Terminus-2 for the 27B
models and Vanillux2 for the 9B models, with $`k{=}5`$. Task selection
and scoring conventions are given in the table captions.

Selection of the “CM” subsets and task lists. Our training generators
cover one domain, computing and math, while TB 3.0 spans many, so we
evaluate on the TB tasks that fall in that domain. To select them we
gave Claude the 74 TB 3.0 task directories together with a description
of our computing-and-math domains and asked it to judge, task by task,
whether the task belongs to that domain even just slightly; we keep the
in-domain tasks that our CPU-only harness can run and grade. This gives
the 22-task TB 3.0 subset, and the 18 of these tasks that remain in
TB 4.0 form the TB 4.0 subset. The computing-and-math split of Agents’
Last Exam has 23 tasks, and ALE-CM keeps the 18 that run in a Linux
Docker container, the sandbox of our training and evaluation harness.
The other five need a full virtual machine, which that sandbox does not
provide. Four boot a Windows 10 guest to analyze Windows binaries or
packet captures with desktop tools such as IDA, Ghidra, x64dbg, and
Wireshark (ghidra_malware_config_extraction_01,
pcap_enterprise_triage_01, tris_crackme, newyear_keygen2); the fifth,
k8s_migration_1, boots an Ubuntu 22 guest to run a Minikube cluster on
Docker, which would require Docker inside our container. The exclusion
follows from the runtime alone, not from task difficulty, and every
model in
Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),
ours and frontier, is scored on the same 18 tasks. The 18 ALE-CM tasks
are listed in
Table [12](#A3.T12 "Table 12 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
and the TB subsets in
Table [13](#A3.T13 "Table 13 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").

Tables [14](#A3.T14 "Table 14 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
and [15](#A3.T15 "Table 15 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")

Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards"),

from the same official per-trial results (5 trials per task, ungraded
trials scored 0, P@1 the per-task mean reward and P@5 the unbiased
pass@5 estimator, macro-averaged over tasks); the full-set P@1
reproduces the leaderboard’s published accuracy to within $`0.01`$ (the
residual comes from trials the leaderboard re-scored after our
snapshot). GLM 5.2 has no TB 4.0 entry, and our own Gemini and Qwen runs
are not leaderboard rows, so they are omitted. Subset scores run
somewhat above full-set scores for most models, but the two agree
closely on how models compare: across models, Pearson $`r`$ (linear
agreement of the scores) is $`0.96`$ (P@1) and $`0.89`$ (P@5) on TB 3.0
and $`0.97`$ and $`0.92`$ on 4.0, and Spearman $`\rho`$ (agreement of
the model ranking) is $`0.94`$/$`0.83`$ and $`0.90`$/$`0.91`$.

For ALE-CM the official Agents’ Last Exam leaderboard publishes only
aggregate scores per model and harness (mean score and pass rate over
the full 152-task benchmark, one run per task) and no per-task results,
so
Table [16](#A3.T16 "Table 16 ‣ C.2 Evaluation Protocol ‣ Appendix C Training and Evaluation Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
sets our own
Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
runs on the 18-task split against the leaderboard’s headline entry for
the same model in the same harness family (its default row, the
reasoning-effort variant with the highest pass rate, retrieved
2026-09-18). Five frontier rows of
Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.3 Progress-Potential Reward Shaping (PPRS) ‣ 3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
have such an entry; Claude Sonnet 5, the Gemini Flash models, and
Qwen3.8-2.4T-A95B do not. Because the two sides differ in harness
configuration and number of rollouts, agreement is looser than on TB:
across the five models Spearman $`\rho`$ is $`0.95`$ for pass rate and
$`0.63`$ for score (Pearson $`r`$ $`0.75`$ and $`0.56`$), with
Gemini 3.1 Pro the main outlier, scoring far higher in our Gemini CLI
runs on the split than on the full benchmark. Close agreement is neither
expected nor our aim: the full benchmark spans many domains beyond
computing and math, such as engineering, finance, multimodal, and 3D
tasks, whereas ALE-CM isolates the one domain our data targets.

are those on which our rollout beats the base rollout;

bash tool with 64 turns; frontier rollouts ran in their native
harnesses. Every task below is also solved by at least one frontier
agent, so these are not tasks on which our model is uniquely capable;
the figures show *how* the rollouts differ. Green marks a decision that
earns grader credit, red one that forfeits it, grey lines summarize
routine steps, and t is the trajectory step.

t11–19 No ancilla file is staged $`\to`$ critical-ancilla rule; installs
the pinned NumPy/SciPy with uv.

t20–33 Trotterized imaginary-time evolution ($`\Delta t{=}0.0002`$,
$`K{=}50000`$) for the ground state, cross-checked against sparse exact
diagonalization: energy $`-12.78491`$ both ways, infidelity
$`1.0{\times}10^{-7}`$; computes $`a(j)`$, $`a(j,k)`$, $`V_{jk}`$,
$`m_{j}`$, $`H^{\prime}`$, $`H_{m}`$ for all 1,024 outcomes by bit-flip
indexing.

t33–36 Verifies shapes and dtypes, unit trace and Hermiticity of every
$`\rho_{1}`$, correlators recomputed from $`\rho_{1}`$; submits.

GPT-5.6 (sol) score: 1.00

 

t3–9 Reads specification and config; the ancilla is unstaged; installs
the pinned dependencies.

t20–21 Exact diagonalization (eigsh) of the spec Hamiltonian; builds
$`H^{\prime}`$ and $`H_{m}`$ with the big-endian conventions; checks
normalization, Hermiticity and correlator consistency.

t23–26 Independent reload check: shapes, dtypes, positivity, Hamiltonian
residual $`8.1{\times}10^{-13}`$, probability sum
$`1-2{\times}10^{-16}`$; submits.

Qwen3.8-27B base score: 0.00

 

t2–12 Reads the specification; installs NumPy/SciPy into a venv.

t14 stage1.py builds the site operators with (idx \>\> b) & 1 where b is
a bit *mask* (512, 256, …), not a bit position, so five of the ten
$`Z_{j}`$ collapse to constants. Its exact diagonalization and its
imaginary-time evolution share this table: “infid ED vs XZ: 3.49e-08”,
$`E_{0}=-17.03`$ where the true value is $`-12.78`$.

t18–32 Writes the protocol; fixes shape bugs caught by
$`|\langle X\rangle|\leq 1`$ checks.

t34–42 “Independent loop-based verification … matches to machine
precision … E0 from saved psi_c: -17.0267 (correct ground state).” The
check reuses the same operator table, so agreement proves nothing;
submits a state with infidelity $`0.79`$ to the reference.

Tev-27B (ours) score: 1.00

 

t2–14 Reads specification and config; installs the pinned NumPy/SciPy
into a uv venv.

t16–42 Implements the Trotterized imaginary-time evolution and the
vectorized protocol over all 1,024 outcomes; fixes a series of
tensor-shape bugs.

t44–50 A sanity print flags “rho1 hermiticity max err: 0.183”; traces it
to its own check ($`\rho_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}`$ instead of
$`\rho_{1}^{\dagger}`$) and confirms
$`\langle Z\rangle,\langle X\rangle`$ read from $`\rho_{1}`$ against
independently computed correlators to $`10^{-15}`$.

t54–58 Writes a separate crosscheck.py that rebuilds $`H`$ from
Kronecker products, takes the ground state with eigsh, re-derives every
quantity and compares the *saved files*: $`p`$ $`1.1{\times}10^{-4}`$,
$`\rho_{1}`$ $`2.9{\times}10^{-4}`$, $`\langle Z\rangle`$
$`5.7{\times}10^{-4}`$, $`\langle X\rangle`$ $`3.8{\times}10^{-4}`$
(tolerances $`10^{-4}`$ / $`10^{-2}`$).

t60–62 Confirms output files and untouched inputs; submits.

Figure 7: Qualitative example on the ALE-CM task
ising_post_measurement_1 (Math & Science). Compute the exact ground
state of a 10-qubit critical transverse-field Ising chain, couple it to
an ancilla copy and derive, for all $`2^{10}`$ projective ancilla
outcomes, the post-measurement probabilities, site-1 reduced density
matrices and one-body correlators; four arrays are graded against a
hidden reference with binary credit. Green marks a decision that secures
the result, red one that forfeits it. All three solving rollouts
cross-check the ground state with an *independent* construction of
$`H`$; the base model’s exact-diagonalization and imaginary-time codes
share one buggy operator table, so their agreement proves nothing.

Claude Fable 5 score: 1.00

 

t13–25 Sets up the venv; writes an assignment-relaxation
branch-and-bound with Carpaneto–Toth subtour branching; fixes a stray
non-ASCII identifier.

t29–35 Tiers 1 and 2 proven optimal in 29 and 657 nodes; tier 3 runs in
the background.

t39–47 Tier 3 lands at 0.49999 %, “just under the 0.005 threshold”
$`\to`$ independently recomputes every tour and cost, then confirms tier
1 against an exact Held–Karp dynamic program.

t51 Writes results.json (tier 3: 172,168 nodes, 100 s); submits.

GPT-5.6 (sol) score: 1.00

 

t6–16 Subtour-branching solver with Hungarian bounds and directed local
search; tier 1 proven optimal in 27 nodes.

t19–31 “The first branching scheme is too redundant on Tier 2” $`\to`$
finds the bug (already-forced arcs re-branched), makes the partition
finite; a Lagrangian bound closes tier 3 immediately.

t36–42 Validates every tour and gap by independent recomputation (tier 3
gap 0.2498 %); cleans the temporary runtime; submits.

Qwen3.8-27B base score: 0.00

 

t8–18 Finds SciPy in the system Python; nearest-neighbor $`+`$ 2-opt
incumbents; benchmarks the Hungarian solver.

t24–32 Assignment-bound branch-and-bound with shape, heap-sign and
int8-overflow bugs; the pure assignment bound (330 vs. optimum 411.7)
leaves a 20 % gap and the 12-city search explodes to 435k nodes.

t40–58 Held–Karp confirms the 12-city optimum; the Lagrangian bound is
rewritten four times (dual sign, multipliers stuck at 0, overflow) until
at t58 it proves tier 1 at the root and bounds tier 3 at 879.98.

t60–64 Turns to incumbent heuristics (subtour patching, Or-opt) instead
of writing the report; the context window overflows with no
results.json.

Tev-27B (ours) score: 1.00

 

t2–22 Sets up the venv; generates the instances; verifies genuine
asymmetry.

t28–36 Best-first branch-and-bound with assignment relaxation: 12 cities
in 13 nodes, 20 cities in 378 nodes; notices the reported bound lags the
optimum and fixes the bound bookkeeping.

t42–58 Held–Karp DP confirms the 12-city optimum; the plain bound stalls
at 1.95 % on 35 cities after 360k nodes $`\to`$ a Lagrangian subgradient
bound closes tier 3 to 0.0 % in 4 nodes.

an independent heuristic for tier 3, and a determinism re-run (diff only
in runtime_seconds).

t86 Submits.
````
