````math
## Appendix D Extended Related Work

Synthetic corpora for coding and terminal agents. The first wave of
agent training data mined real repositories: SWE-bench ([Jimenez et al.,
2024](#bib.bib1)) and SWE-Gym ([Pan et al., 2025](#bib.bib32)) collect
GitHub issues with their tests, and SWE-rebench ([Badertdinov et al.,
2025](#bib.bib33)) and Skywork-SWE ([Zeng et al., 2025](#bib.bib28))
automate the mining. The second wave synthesizes tasks: SWE-smith ([Yang
et al., 2025a](#bib.bib31)) injects bugs into working repositories,
SWE-Flow ([Zhang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib34)) strips a repository along
its runtime dependency graph, and R2E-Gym ([Jain et al.,
2025](#bib.bib35)) back-translates problem statements from commits.
Terminal corpora follow the same pattern at larger scale, either with a
language model writing tasks, containers, and tests that are then
filtered for solvability, as in Endless Terminals ([Gandhi et al.,
2026](#bib.bib40)), SETA ([Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)), and
recursive solution extension ([Li et al., 2026a](#bib.bib42))
(TMAX ([Ivison et al., 2026](#bib.bib29)) skips this filter and lets RL
drop unsolved tasks), or by reconstructing environments from agent
trajectories, as in CLI-Gym ([Lin et al., 2026](#bib.bib41)) and
Terminal-Universe ([Wu et al., 2026](#bib.bib30)). All of these scale
the number of *instances*: each instance is pull-request-sized or a
single session, its verifier is a test suite that caps the complexity a
task can have, and generation depends on a model whose output must be
validated after the fact. We scale *environments*: a coding agent writes
each generator once, the authors review it, and instantiating it
involves no model; verification is against the latent world rather than
a test suite. Closer in spirit is the programmatic synthesis of
executable, stateful tool environments, AutoForge ([Cai et al.,
2025](#bib.bib70)), EnvScaler ([Song et al., 2026](#bib.bib58)),
ScaleEnv ([Tu et al., 2026](#bib.bib68)), Agent-World ([Dong et al.,
2026](#bib.bib69)), EnvFactory ([Xu et al., 2026](#bib.bib67)), and
LOGIGEN ([Zeng et al., 2026b](#bib.bib71)), which also treats the
environment as the unit of scale ([Fang et al., 2025](#bib.bib57));
these are API worlds for short tool-use episodes, and only LOGIGEN emits
a progress signal, a per-turn reward from the database’s proximity to
its target state. Two findings from that line carry over: more
environments alone do not help without difficulty control ([Zhu et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib60)), and coverage rather than count is what drives
transfer ([Chen et al., 2026a](#bib.bib77); [Chen et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib78); [Xu et al., 2026](#bib.bib67)). Our environments
differ from this family on the two properties it largely lacks: each
spans a long, multi-stage workflow across math and science, ML, and
coding and systems domains rather than a short tool-use episode, and
every one ships the progress potential $`\Phi_{W}`$ of
§[3](#S3 "3 Dense Reward for Long-Horizon RL ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")
rather than a bare success condition, so the difficulty control and
dense signal these two findings call for come from the same generator
that supplies coverage.

Backward construction. Latent-world-first synthesis belongs to a long
line of methods that derive a task from its solution. Denoising
objectives corrupt text and learn to restore it ([Lewis et al.,
2020](#bib.bib43)); the data-cleaning community injects errors of
controlled repairability into clean databases to evaluate repair
algorithms ([Arocena et al., 2015](#bib.bib63)) and corrupts synthetic
person records to benchmark record linkage ([Tran et al.,
2013](#bib.bib64)); mutation and bug injection corrupt working
code ([Yang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib31); [Hariharan et al.,
2025](#bib.bib36)); CLI-Gym rewinds a healthy environment to an earlier
broken state ([Lin et al., 2026](#bib.bib41)); instruction
back-translation derives prompts from answers ([Li et al.,
2023](#bib.bib44); [Wu et al., 2024](#bib.bib45)); and procedural
generators with rule-based verifiers supply unlimited single-turn
reasoning problems whose answers are known by construction ([Stojanovski
et al., 2025](#bib.bib46); [Chen et al., 2025a](#bib.bib47); [Liu et
al., 2025](#bib.bib48); [Li et al., 2025](#bib.bib49)). In every case
the corrupted object is a single artifact graded by a pre-existing
oracle, or a one-step puzzle. We corrupt a rendering of an entire world
into many mutually constraining files of different formats, and the
oracle is the world itself; this is what lets exactness, and the ground
truth of every intermediate stage, survive a multi-hour horizon. In the
taxonomy of [Zeng et al. (2026a)](#bib.bib66), generation pipelines
anchor on the environment, the task, the trajectory, or a blueprint that
coordinates them; anchoring on the verified terminal state itself is
what removes the artifact drift among task, environment, and verifier
that separately generated factors invite ([Ivanov and Rana,
2026](#bib.bib72)). Concretely, we anchor one level earlier than any
object in that taxonomy: the latent world $`W`$ itself, so that the task
$`x`$, the input tree $`s_{0}`$, the strict verifier
$`\operatorname{ver}_{W}`$, and the progress potential $`\Phi_{W}`$
(Eq. ([1](#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ 2.1 Latent-World-First Task Synthesis ‣ 2 Long-Horizon Environment Synthesis ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards")))
are four views of one object rather than four artifacts a blueprint must
keep synchronized after the fact. CLI-Gym is the closest of these in
mechanism, but it rewinds a single running environment to an earlier
state and stops at one pass/fail check, with no signal over the stages
in between.

World-state verification, environment scaling, and long-horizon
benchmarks. Verifying an agent against the final state of a simulated
world rather than against its transcript is established in
AppWorld ([Trivedi et al., 2024](#bib.bib50)), $`\tau`$-bench ([Yao et
al., 2025](#bib.bib51)), WebArena ([Zhou et al., 2024a](#bib.bib52)),
and OSWorld ([Xie et al., 2024](#bib.bib53)), all with hand-authored
tasks for evaluation. Recent work generates the world too:
AgentMercury ([Jeong and Yoon, 2026](#bib.bib54)) has a model author
persistent business worlds with executable invariants, and [Zhang et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib55) derive dense rewards from progress predicates over
generated web sites; neither uses a declared corruption, and both depend
on a model to write the world. A growing literature argues that
environments are the bottleneck of agentic RL ([Xi et al.,
2025](#bib.bib56); [Fang et al., 2025](#bib.bib57); [Song et al.,
2026](#bib.bib58); [Zhang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib59); [Gandhi et al.,
2026](#bib.bib40)), with the caveat that more environments alone do not
help without difficulty control ([Zhu et al., 2026b](#bib.bib60)). Our
thesis is narrower: the environments worth scaling are those whose
latent world supplies both the oracle and the progress signal.
Long-horizon benchmarks with partial credit, TheAgentCompany ([Xu et
al., 2024](#bib.bib25)), Long-Horizon Terminal-Bench ([Li et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib27)), PaperBench ([Starace et al., 2025](#bib.bib26)),
MLE-bench ([Chan et al., 2025](#bib.bib61)), and ALE-Bench ([Imajuku et
al., 2025](#bib.bib62)), target the regime we train for, but their
checkpoints are written per task by hand or graded by a model, and at
tens of instances they are evaluation sets.

Sparse reward and dense signal in agentic RL. Group-relative methods
discard rollout groups with identical rewards ([Yu et al.,
2025](#bib.bib3); [He et al., 2025](#bib.bib7); [Ivison et al.,
2026](#bib.bib29)), schedule prompts by difficulty ([Yang et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib8); [Sha et al., 2026](#bib.bib9)), or seed groups with
expert prefixes ([Zhang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib10)); all assume enough
solvable prompts to fill a batch. A complementary line moves the tasks
rather than the reward, generating or selecting them near the learner’s
current success band ([Florensa et al., 2018](#bib.bib76); [Guo et al.,
2025](#bib.bib74); [Chen et al., 2025b](#bib.bib73); [Wolf et al.,
2026](#bib.bib75)); this keeps groups informative but caps difficulty at
what the current policy can sometimes finish. Denser signals come from
learned or judged step rewards ([Cui et al., 2025](#bib.bib14);
[Choudhury, 2025](#bib.bib13); [Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib16); [Zhou et
al., 2025](#bib.bib17); [Zhou et al., 2024b](#bib.bib18); [Gunjal et
al., 2025](#bib.bib11)), from advantages over recurring states ([Feng et
al., 2025](#bib.bib15)), or from exact but proxy quantities: similarity
to an oracle patch ([Wei et al., 2025](#bib.bib21)), the fraction of
tests passed ([Du et al., 2025](#bib.bib22); [Shen et al.,
2026](#bib.bib23)), the count of passing assertions ([Yang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib24)), compiler diagnostics ([Zhang and Lanser
Contributors, 2025](#bib.bib20)), path-centric credit for evidence
gathering ([Xia et al., 2026](#bib.bib79)), or symbolic oracles in
closed domains ([Yuan et al., 2026](#bib.bib19)). Test-fraction rewards
are the closest to ours and are what lets SETA and T1 train on terminal
tasks, but they score only what the terminal tests probe, are not
monotone in workflow progress, and carry no notion of regression. Our
potential is exact progress against the latent world at every workflow
stage, anchored so that $`\Phi=1`$ exactly when the verifier passes, and
free for every seed. A recent finding shapes how such a signal should be
used: a shaping term that is not aligned with the task collapses GRPO
agents, because in all-fail groups it becomes the whole
objective ([Wang, 2026](#bib.bib12)). Unlike the learned or judged step
rewards above, $`\Phi`$ needs no trained critic or LM judge; its
relation to classical potential-based shaping ([Ng et al.,
1999](#bib.bib5)) is discussed in
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Dense Reward Details ‣ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon AgenticEnvironments with Dense Progress Rewards").
````
